# CHAPTER I

## INNER STATE AND OUTER FORM

"Human Nature" is a term used very frequently, yet it is understood by very few. Most people have a general idea of what they and others mean when they use this phrase, but very few are able to give an immediate definition or explain what they believe constitutes the true essence of the idea. We believe that the first step toward correctly understanding any subject is to become familiar with its primary terms. Therefore, we will begin our study of Human Nature by examining the phrase itself.

"Human," naturally, means "relating to humanity or mankind." Consequently, Human Nature means the nature of human beings. In this context, "Nature" means: "The natural mental disposition <note>original: "disposition of mind"</note> of any person; their temperament; personal character; or individual—"

<vocab>Human Nature, mankind, natural disposition, personal character</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the study of Human Nature by emphasizing the importance of defining core terms, specifically "human" and "nature." It argues that while the phrase is common, its true essence is rarely defined clearly by the average person.</summary>
<keywords>Human Nature, definitions, character, temperament, human behavior, terminology</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the relationship between internal mental states and their external physical manifestations, beginning mid-word with "[sev]eral phases."</meta>

# INNER STATE AND OUTER FORM

...several phases, that is: (1) the phase of Inner States; and (2) the phase of Outer Forms. These two phases, however, are not separate or opposed to each other, but are complementary aspects of the same thing. There is always an action and reaction between the Inner State and the Outer Form—between the Inner Feeling and the Outer Expression. If we know the particular Inner State, we may infer the appropriate Outer Form; and if we know the Outer Form, we may infer the Inner State.

That the Inner State affects the Outer Form is a fact generally acknowledged by humanity, for it is in strict accordance with the general experience of the human race. We know that certain mental states will result in imparting to the <term>countenance</term> <note>original: "countenance"; a person's face or facial expression</note> certain lines and expressions appropriate to them; certain peculiarities of <term>carriage</term> <note>original: "carriage"; the way a person carries their body; posture</note> and manner, voice and <term>demeanor</term> <note>original: "demeanor"; outward behavior or bearing</note>. The <term>facial characteristics</term> <note>the features or appearance of the face</note>, manner, walk, voice, and gestures of the <term>miser</term> <note>original: "miser"; a person who hoards wealth and lives miserably to save money</note> will be recognized as entirely different from those of the generous person; those of the coward differ materially from those of the brave man; those of the vain are distinguished from

<summary>This page explains the reciprocal relationship between "Inner States" (feelings) and "Outer Forms" (physical expression), arguing that one can always be inferred from the other. It notes how character traits like greed, bravery, or vanity are physically manifested in a person’s face, posture, and voice.</summary>

<keywords>Inner State, Outer Form, psychology, facial expression, body language, character traits, physiognomy, mental states</keywords>

...those of the modest. We know that certain mental attitudes will produce the corresponding physical expressions of a smile, a frown, an open hand, a clenched fist, an erect spine, or bowed shoulders, respectively. We also know that certain feelings will cause the eye to sparkle or grow dim, and the voice to become resonant and positive or to become husky and weak, according to the nature of those feelings.

Professor William James says: "It is quite impossible for me to imagine what kind of emotion of fear would be left if the feeling of trembling lips, weakened limbs, goosebumps, and <term>visceral stirrings</term> <note>original: "visceral stirrings"; physical sensations felt in the internal organs, like a "gut feeling"</note> were not present. Can one imagine a state of rage and picture no <term>ebullition</term> <note>original: "ebullition"; a sudden outburst of emotion, literally a "boiling over"</note> in the chest, no flushing of the face, no dilation of the nostrils, no clenching of the teeth, and no impulse toward vigorous action, but in their place limp muscles, calm breathing, and a placid face?"

Professor Halleck says: "All emotions have well-defined muscular expressions. Darwin has written an excellent work entitled *The Expression of the Emotions in Man and Animals*, to which students must refer for a detailed account of such expression. A very..."

<summary>This page argues that emotions are inseparable from their physical manifestations, citing psychologists William James and Reuben Halleck, as well as Charles Darwin's work on emotional expression.</summary>
<keywords>William James, Reuben Halleck, Charles Darwin, emotional expression, physical manifestations, visceral stirrings, ebullition, psychology of emotion</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how internal emotional states are reflected in outward physical expressions, building on the works of Darwin and psychologists mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

## INNER STATE AND OUTER FORM

A few examples must suffice here. In all positive or uplifting emotions, the eyebrows, the eyelids, the nostrils, and the corners of the mouth are raised. In sad or depressing emotions, the opposite occurs. This general statement contains so much truth that a careful observer can read a large part of a human being's personal history written in their face. For this reason, many <term>phrenologists</term> <note>original: "phrenologists"; practitioners of a now-discredited theory that the shape of the skull indicates mental traits and character.</note> have wisely turned into <term>physiognomists</term> <note>original: "physiognomists"; people who study the practice of judging character or personality from outward appearance, especially the face.</note>. 

Grief is expressed by raising the inner ends of the eyebrows, drawing down the corners of the mouth, and creating horizontal wrinkles across the middle of the forehead. In Tierra del Fuego, a group of natives conveyed the idea to Charles Darwin that a certain man was depressed by pulling down their cheeks to make their faces look long. Joy is expressed by drawing the corners of the mouth backward and upward. The upper lip rises and pulls the cheeks upward, forming wrinkles under the eyes. The lifting of the upper lip and the nostrils expresses contempt. 

A skillful observer can frequently tell if one person admires another. In this case, the eyebrows are raised, revealing a brightening eye and a relaxed expression; sometimes a gentle smile plays about...

<summary>This page describes specific facial muscle movements associated with emotions like joy, grief, and contempt, noting how facial expressions can reveal a person's history.</summary>
<keywords>facial expressions, physiognomy, phrenology, Charles Darwin, Tierra del Fuego, grief, joy, contempt, admiration</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how internal emotional states are reflected in outward physical expressions. It concludes the description of how the mind affects the body ("Inner upon Outer") and introduces the reciprocal concept: how physical actions influence mental states ("Outer upon Inner").</meta>

->HUMAN NATURE<-

...the mouth. Blushing is merely the physical expression of certain emotions. We notice the expression of emotion more in the face <note>original: "countenance"</note>, because the effects are more plainly visible there; but the muscles of the entire body, the vital organs, and the <term>viscera</term> <note>original: "viscera"; the internal organs in the main cavities of the body, such as the heart, lungs, and intestines.</note> also serve as vehicles for expression.

These points only need to be mentioned to be recognized and accepted. This describes the action of the Inner (the mind) upon the Outer (the body). There is, however, a reaction of the Outer upon the Inner. While this is equally true, it is not as generally recognized or admitted. We think it is important to briefly call your attention to this, because this correspondence between the Inner and the Outer—the reaction as well as the action—must be appreciated so that the entire meaning and content of the subject of Human Nature may be fully grasped.

To help you understand how the *reaction* of the Outer Form affects the Inner State, we ask you to consider the following opinions from well-known and accepted authorities of the <term>New Psychology</term> <note>original: "New Psychology"; a movement in the late 19th and early 20th centuries that sought to make psychology an experimental, physiological science rather than a branch of philosophy.</note>. They address the established fact that a physical expression related to a...

<summary>This page explains that while emotions (the Inner) clearly affect our physical bodies (the Outer), the reverse is also true: our physical state and expressions can influence our internal emotions.</summary>
<keywords>New Psychology, Inner and Outer correspondence, physical expression, emotional reaction, viscera</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that physical expressions can trigger corresponding mental states. It provides evidence from psychological authorities to support the theory that the relationship between mind and body is a two-way street.</meta>

### INNER STATE AND OUTER FORM

...mental state will, if voluntarily induced, tend in turn to induce the mental state appropriate to it. We have used these quotations in other books of this series, but will include them here because they have a direct bearing upon the particular subject before us, and because they provide direct and reliable authority for the statements we have just made. We ask you to consider them carefully, for they express a most important truth.

<term>Prof. Halleck</term> <note>Reuben Post Halleck (1859–1936) was an American psychologist and educator known for his work on how the nervous system relates to education.</note> says:
> “By inducing an expression, we can often cause its allied emotion. . . . Actors have frequently testified to the fact that emotion will arise if they go through the appropriate muscular movements. In talking to a character on the stage, if they clench their fists and frown, they often find themselves becoming really angry; if they start with counterfeit laughter, they find themselves growing cheerful. A German professor says that he cannot walk with a schoolgirl’s <term>mincing</term> <note>original: "mincing"; walking with short, affectedly dainty or elegant steps.</note> step and air without feeling frivolous.”

<term>Prof. William James</term> <note>William James (1842–1910) was a pioneering American psychologist and philosopher, often called the "Father of American psychology."</note> says:
> “Whistling to keep up courage is no mere figure of speech.

<summary>This page explains that deliberately adopting a physical expression or posture can actually trigger the corresponding emotion within the mind. It cites psychologists Reuben Post Halleck and William James to support the idea that physical actions influence internal mental states.</summary>
<keywords>psychology, physical expression, emotions, Reuben Post Halleck, William James, body-mind connection, facial expressions, mental state</keywords>

## HUMAN NATURE

On the other hand, if you sit all day in a moping posture, sigh, and reply to everything with a dismal voice, your melancholy <note>original: "melancholy"; a deep, pensive, and long-lasting sadness.</note> will linger. If we wish to conquer undesirable emotional tendencies in ourselves, we must persistently <note>original: "assiduously"; showing great care and perseverance.</note>—and at first, even with cold deliberation—perform the outward movements of those opposite moods which we wish to cultivate. Smooth your brow, brighten your eyes, pull back your shoulders <note>original: "contract the dorsal rather than the ventral aspect of the frame"; meaning to stand up straight with the back muscles engaged rather than slouching forward.</note>, speak in a cheerful tone <note>original: "speak in a major key"</note>, and offer a kind compliment; your heart must indeed be frozen if it does not gradually begin to thaw."

<term>Dr. Wood Hutchinson</term> <note>Woods Hutchinson (1862–1930) was a prominent American physician and medical writer known for popularizing health and biology in the early 20th century.</note> says: "The extent to which muscular contractions influence emotions, as <term>Prof. James</term> <note>William James, the psychologist mentioned on the previous page, who proposed that physical changes often precede the feeling of emotion.</note> has suggested, can be easily tested by a simple little experiment on a group of the smallest muscles we can control: those that move the eyeball. Choose a time when you are sitting quietly in your room, free from all distractions. Then stand up, assume a comfortable position, look upward, and hold your eyes in that position for thirty seconds. Instantly and instinctively, you will become aware of a tendency toward..."

<summary>This page explores the "James-Lange theory" of emotion, which suggests that physical actions—like standing straight or looking upward—can deliberately trigger positive mental states. It introduces a practical experiment involving eye muscles to demonstrate the physiological link between movement and mood.</summary>

...of reverential, devotional, and contemplative ideas and thoughts. Then turn your eyes sideways, glancing directly to the right or to the left through half-closed lids. Within thirty seconds, feelings of suspicion, uneasiness, or dislike will arise spontaneously <note>original: "unbidden"; without being consciously willed or invited.</note> in your mind. Turn your eyes to one side and slightly downward, and thoughts of jealousy or flirtation <note>original: "coquetry"; flirtatious or playful behavior.</note> are likely to arise. Direct your gaze downward toward the floor, and you will likely drift into a state of deep thought <note>original: "reverie"; a state of being pleasantly lost in one's thoughts; a daydream.</note> or abstraction.

<term>Prof. Maudsley</term> <note>Henry Maudsley (1835–1918) was a prominent British psychiatrist and a pioneer in the study of the relationship between the mind and body.</note> says: "Specific muscular action is not merely a reflection <note>original: "exponent"; an indicator or representative sign.</note> of emotion <note>original: "passion"; used here to mean any strong feeling or emotional state.</note>, but truly an essential part of it. If we try to call up a different emotion in our minds while our facial features are fixed in the expression of another, we shall find it impossible to do so."

We state the fact of this reaction of the outer physical self upon the inner state, supported by these quotations from authorities, not for the purpose of teaching our readers how to train their emotions through physical means—for while this subject is highly important, it forms no part of this particular...

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header># HUMAN NATURE</header>

...subject under our present consideration—but it is also so that the student may realize the close relationship existing between the Inner State and the Outer Form. In their constant action and reaction, these two elements or phases manifest the phenomena of Human Nature. A knowledge of each, and both together, gives us the key that will open the door to our understanding of Human Nature.

Let us now draw your attention to an illustration that embodies both principles—the Inner and the Outer—and the action and reaction between them, as described by that master of subtle reasoning <note>original: "ratiocination"; the process of exact thinking or logical reasoning.</note>, Edgar Allan Poe. In his story “The Purloined Letter,” Poe tells of a boy at school who attained great proficiency in the game of “even or odd.” In this game, one player strives to guess whether the marbles held in the hand of his opponent are an odd or even number. The boy’s strategy was to gauge the intelligence of his opponent regarding how they chose to change their patterns. As Poe says: “this lay in mere observation and measurement <note>original: "admeasurement"; the act of measuring or determining the proportions of something.</note> of the cleverness <note>original: "astuteness"; the ability to accurately assess situations or people and use this to one's advantage.</note> of his opponents.” 

Poe describes the process as follows: “For example, a complete <note>original: "arrant"; utter or absolute (usually used to emphasize a negative quality).</note> fool <note>original: "simpleton"; a person lacking in common sense or intelligence.</note> is...

<summary>This page explains the interconnectedness of internal thoughts and external physical states, using a famous example from Edgar Allan Poe's "The Purloined Letter" to illustrate how observing outward behavior can reveal inward intelligence.</summary>
<keywords>Inner State, Outer Form, Edgar Allan Poe, The Purloined Letter, psychology of observation, reasoning, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the quote from Edgar Allan Poe’s "The Purloined Letter," illustrating how a successful "reasoner" identifies their own mind with that of their opponent to predict their actions.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>INNER STATE AND OUTER FORM</header>

his opponent, and, holding up his closed hand, asks, "Are they even or odd?" Our schoolboy replies, "Odd," and loses; but upon the second trial he wins, for he then says to himself, "The fool <note>original: "simpleton"</note> had an even number during the first trial, and his amount of cunning is just enough to make him switch to odd for the second; I will therefore guess odd." He guesses and wins. 

Now, with an opponent one degree smarter than the first, he would have reasoned this way: "This fellow sees that in the first instance I guessed odd. In the second round, his first impulse will be to propose to himself a simple variation from even to odd, just as the first fool did. But then, a second thought will suggest to him that this is too simple a change, and finally, he will decide to keep them even as before. I will therefore guess even." He guesses even and wins.

Poe continues by stating that this "is merely an identification of the reasoner's intellect with that of his opponent. Upon asking the boy by what means he achieved the thorough identification that led to his success, I received the following answer: 'When I wish to find out how wise, or how...

<summary>This page uses Edgar Allan Poe’s story of a schoolboy’s success at the game of "even or odd" to demonstrate how logic and psychological identification allow one person to predict another's behavior. It emphasizes the importance of matching one’s own intellect to the level of their opponent's cunning.</summary>

<keywords>Edgar Allan Poe, even or odd, reasoning, intellect, identification, psychology, game theory, logic, The Purloined Letter</keywords>

# HUMAN NATURE

...stupid, how good, or how wicked someone is, or what their thoughts are at the moment, *I shape my facial expression to match theirs as closely as possible. Then, I wait to see what thoughts or feelings arise in my own mind or heart that seem to match that expression.*' This schoolboy's response is the foundation for all the fake depth <note>original: "spurious profundity"</note> that has been attributed to thinkers like Rochefoucauld, La Bruyère, Machiavelli, and Campanella." <note>These are famous European philosophers and political theorists known for their observations on human behavior and strategy.</note>

In this study of Human Nature, we will discuss the "Outer Form" extensively. However, we ask the reader to always remember that the Outer Form is the expression and manifestation of one's "Inner State." This Inner State might be hidden <note>original: "latent"</note> and inactive within the depths of the subconscious mind, or active and energetic <note>original: "dynamic"</note> in conscious expression. Just as Professor James <note>William James (1842–1910), a pioneering American psychologist who argued that physical reactions often precede emotions.</note> strongly argues, we cannot imagine an inner feeling or emotion without its corresponding outward physical expression. Likewise, it is impossible to imagine the outward expressions usually linked with a specific feeling or emo-

<summary>This page concludes a quote from Poe about using facial mimicry to understand others' thoughts and introduces the core theme that outward physical forms are direct reflections of inner psychological states.</summary>

<keywords>physiognomy, psychology, Outer Form, Inner State, Edgar Allan Poe, William James, facial expression, subconscious</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the inseparable link between physical expression and internal emotion, specifically referencing the psychological theories of William James regarding how bodily changes create feelings.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>

### INNER STATE AND OUTER FORM

emotion <note>The text continues from "emo-" on the previous page.</note> without its corresponding inner state. Whether or not one of these—the outer form or the inner state—is the cause of the other (and if so, *which one* is the cause and which is the effect) does not need to concern us here. In fact, it seems more reasonable to accept the theory that they are correlated <note>term: "correlated" — having a mutual relationship where one thing affects or depends on another.</note> and appear simultaneously. Many careful thinkers have held that action and reaction are practically the same thing—merely two different sides of the same reality. If this is true, then indeed, when we are studying the Outer Form of Human Nature, we are studying psychology just as much as when we are studying Inner States. 

As we have seen, Professor William James <note>William James (1842–1910) was a pioneering American psychologist and philosopher who argued that physical reactions often precede the conscious feeling of an emotion.</note> insisted in his works on psychology that it is relevant to consider the outward expressions of inner feelings and emotions. This same authority speaks even more forcefully on this aspect of the subject as follows:

> “In the more basic <note>original: "coarser"</note> emotions, the feeling results from the bodily expression. . . . My theory is that the bodily changes follow directly the perception of the exciting fact, and that our feeling of those same changes as <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

He who learns the alphabet of Outer Form may read page after page of the book of Human Nature.

# CHAPTER II

## THE INNER PHASE: CHARACTER

Do you know what “character” is? The word itself, in its derivation and original usage, means: “a stamp, mark or sign, engraved or stamped.” <note>The word originates from the Greek *charaktēr*, which referred to an engraving tool or the distinctive mark left by a seal. This implies that character is something deeply etched into a person’s nature.</note> As time passed the term was applied to the personal peculiarities of individuals, and was defined as: “the personal qualities or attributes of a person; the distinguishing traits of a person.” Later the term was extended to mean: “the part enacted by anyone in a play.” In the common usage of the term we seek to convey an idea in which each and all of the above stated meanings are combined. A man’s character is the result of impressions made upon his own mind, or those of the race. <note>The "impressions of the race" is a reference to the early 20th-century concept of collective or hereditary memory—the idea that humans inherit certain mental tendencies from their ancestors.</note> It is also the sum of his personal qualities and attributes. It is also, in a sense, the part he plays in the great drama of life.

Each man’s character has its inner phase consisting of the accumulated impressions of the past which seek to manifest in the present. And, likewise, the character of each

There is an infinite possibility of combination of the elements that go to make up character. This is in accordance with what appears to be a universal law of nature, for there are no two blades of grass exactly alike, nor two grains of sand bearing an exact resemblance to each other. Nature seems to seek after and to manifest variety of form and quality. But, still, just as we may classify all things, animate and inanimate, into general classes and then into subordinate ones—each <term>genus<note>A biological category used to group species that share similar characteristics.</note></term> and each <term>species</term> having its particular characteristics, qualities and attributes, so we may, and do, classify human character into general classes and then into particular subdivisions into which each individual is found to fit. This fact makes it possible for us to study Human Nature as a science.

The modern tendency <note>The author refers to the psychological trends of the late 19th and early 20th centuries.</note> is to overestimate the effects of heredity and environment in forming character; but, on the other hand, we must not underestimate them. The child of a <term>Hottentot<note>A historical, now-offensive term used by Europeans to refer to the Khoekhoe people of Southern Africa. As for environment, it is folly to deny its influence. Take two young persons of equal ability, similar tastes, and the same heredity, and place them one in a small village, and the other in a great metropolis, and keep them there until middle-age, and we will see the influence of environment. The two may be equally happy and contented, and may possess the same degree of book-education, but, nevertheless, their experiences will have been so different that the character of the two individuals must be different. The effect of environment arises from **Suggestion** <note>In the psychology of the late 19th and early 20th centuries, "Suggestion" was a key term describing how external ideas or the influence of others can shape a person's thoughts and actions, often without them realizing it.</note>, that great moulding and creative principle of the mind. But, without going further into the question of the elements which go toward forming character, let us take our position firmly upon the fact that each individual is stamped with the impression of a special character—a character all his own. Each has his own *character* or part to play in the great drama of life. The character of some seems fixed and unchangeable, while that of others is seen to be in the process of change. ## CHAPTER III

### THE OUTER PHASE: PERSONALITY

Just as *character* is the inner phase of Human Nature, so is *personality* its outer phase. To many the two terms are synonymous, but analysis will show the shades of difference between them. A man’s *character* is his inner self, while his *personality* is the outward indication of his self. The word, in this sense, is defined as: “That which constitutes the personal traits of a person, as his manner, conduct, habits, appearance, and other observable personal peculiarities.”

The word is derived from the Latin word, *persona* <note>original: "persona." In ancient theater, these were masks designed to project the actor's voice.</note>, meaning, “a mask used by play-actors,” which in turn was derived from the two words *through* <note>original: "per"</note> and *to sound* <note>original: "sono"</note>, or combined, “to sound through.” And the derivation of the term really gives us an idea of its inner meaning, for the personality is really the mask worn by the character, and through which it sounds, speaks, or manifests itself. # CHAPTER IV

## THE TEMPERAMENTS

The student of **Human Nature** soon discovers that among men, as among the animals, there is to be observed a great variety of "quality," and various classes of "temperament." <note>The theory of "temperaments" traditionally stems from the ancient Greek belief in the four humors, but by the 19th century, it was used by character analysts to categorize personality types based on physical build and "vitality."</note> Among cattle we notice great differences of form which differences indicate certain qualities inherent in the beast. Certain qualities are recognized by their outward forms as being indicative of sturdiness, staying-qualities, strong vitality, etc., which render their possessor valuable for draught oxen. <note>original: "draught"—meaning animals used for pulling heavy loads, such as plows or wagons.</note> Other qualities indicate the value of another animal for meat producing. Others, the production of large quantities of milk. Others, prolific breeding. And, so on, each set of qualities being recognized by its outward form and being taken into consideration by breeders. In the same way, breeders recognize certain qualities in horses which they take advantage of in breeding for the strength of draught horses; the speed of thoroughbred runners and trotters; the docil-

In this lowest degree we find individuals showing all the outward signs of being coarse-grained, vulgar, of low tastes, brutal instincts, and manifesting the signs of lack of refinement and culture. Persons of low Quality are found in all walks of life. Some of those possessing wealth and education belong to this class, and are never able to counterfeit the reality. <term>Quality: in this context, a 19th-century psychological term referring to the perceived "fineness" or "coarseness" of a person's physical and mental makeup.</term> is a matter of "soul," and not of wealth, education or material advantages. A greyhound and a hyena <note>The author uses animal comparisons common in physiognomy—the study of character through physical features—where the sleek greyhound represents high-bred elegance and the hyena represents base, scavenging instincts.</note> give us animal symbols of Quality, high and low.

We meet many instances in which the individual is of too high Quality for his environment, occupation or place in life. Such individuals suffer keenly and are to be pitied. They incline toward high ideals and are wounded and discouraged by the grossness which they see on all sides. Those individuals of an average degree of Quality of course fit into the usual environment far better than those above or below them in the scale. We also meet individuals of low Quality in surroundings in which they are out of place—we see many instances of "pigs <note>The text breaks off here. Hippocrates, the ancient Greek philosopher-physician <note>Hippocrates (c. 460 – c. 370 BCE) is often called the "Father of Medicine." His theory suggested that human health and personality were governed by the balance of four bodily fluids, or "humors."</note> (B. C. 468—367) held to the existence of four temperaments, which he attributed to certain qualities of the blood and the several secretions of the body such as the bile, etc. While his theory was rejected by later investigators, his classification continued until very recently under the name of (1) the Sanguine; (2) the Lymphatic or Phlegmatic; (3) the Choleric or Bilious; and (4) the Melancholic temperaments, respectively. As a matter of general information on the subject we herewith give the old classification with the attributes of each class:

The *Sanguine* temperament <note>Derived from the Latin "sanguis" (blood), this temperament was historically associated with an abundance of blood.</note> was held to be characterized by red or light-brown hair, blue eyes, a fair or ruddy complexion, large arteries and veins, a full and rapid pulse, slight perspiration, impatience of heat, febrile tendency <note>A "febrile tendency" refers to a predisposition to developing fevers.</note>, and lively and cheerful temper, excitable passions, a warm, ardent, impulsive disposition, and a liking for active pursuits;

The *Lymphatic*, or *Phlegmatic* temperament <note>Historically associated with an excess of "phlegm," thought to make a person cool and composed.</note> was held to be characterized by light,

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>THE TEMPERAMENTS</header>

sandy, or whitish hair, light grey eyes, pallid complexion, skin almost devoid of hair, flabby tissues, much perspiration, small blood-vessels, a feeble and slow pulse, want of energy, lack of activity, deficient spirit and vividness;

The *Choleric* or *Bilious* temperament <note>The term "choleric" originates from the Greek word for yellow bile. This temperament was traditionally associated with a fiery, ambitious, and easily angered nature.</note> was held to be characterized by black hair often curling, black or hazel eyes, and dark but ruddy complexion, hairy skin, strong full pulse, firm muscles, great activity and positiveness, strength of character, and an active brain.

The *Melancholic* temperament <note>From the Greek "melaina chole," meaning "black bile." Historically, those with this temperament were thought to be prone to sadness, but also to deep thought and artistic creativity.</note> was held to be characterized by black hair, black or hazel eyes, a dark leaden <note>A "leaden" complexion refers to a dull, heavy, or grayish skin tone.</note> complexion, pulse slow and feeble, and a disposition toward study, poetry, literature, and sentiment.

Some later authorities added a fifth temperament, called the *Nervous* temperament <note>This category was added in the 18th and 19th centuries as physicians began to prioritize the brain and nerves over the ancient "humors" or bodily fluids.</note>, which was held to be characterized by a medium complexion, large brain, small physical frame, fineness of organization, thin hair, finely cut features, quick lively disposition, intellectual tastes and tendencies, sensitive nature, high capacity for enjoyment and suffering.

# HUMAN NATURE

The latest authorities, however, discarded the old classification and adopted one more simple although fully as comprehensive. The new classification recognizes three classes of temperament, viz: (1) the **Vital**; (2) the **Motive**; and (3) the **Mental**, the characteristics of which are held to be as follows:

The **Vital** temperament <note>This classification refers to the physical constitution focused on the organs that sustain life and nutrition.</note> has its basis in the predominance of the <term>nutritive system</term> <note>The collective organs involved in digestion and the distribution of nutrients.</note>, including the blood-vessels, <term>lymphatics</term> <note>The network of vessels that help rid the body of toxins and transport immune cells.</note> and the glands. Its organs are the heart, lungs, stomach, liver, bowels, and the entire internal vital system. It is characterized by a large, broad frame; broad shoulders; deep chest; full round abdomen; round plump limbs; short thick neck; comparatively small hands and feet; full face; flushed and <term>florid</term> <note>A reddish or rosy complexion, traditionally viewed as a sign of robust health or a "full-blooded" nature.</note> cheeks; and general “well fed” appearance. 

Those in whom it is predominant are fond of out-of-door exercise, although not of hard work; crave the “good things of life;” fond of sport, games and play; love variety of entertainment and amusement; are affectionate; love praise and flattery; prefer concrete rather than abstract subjects of thought; look out for themselves; are selfish, but yet “good

Persons of the **Vital** temperament may have either fair or dark complexion, but in either case the cheeks and face are apt to be ruddy and flushed. This temperament is particularly noticeable in women, a large proportion of whom belong to its class. We have noticed that a large number of railroad engineers and policemen are of this temperament.

### BALANCED TEMPERAMENTS

The best authorities agree in the belief that the Balanced Temperament is the most desirable. That is, the condition in which the three temperaments <note>The author refers to the Vital, Motive, and Mental temperaments discussed earlier in the manuscript.</note> balance each other perfectly, so that the weak points of each are remedied by the strong points of the others, and the extremes of each are neutralized and held in check by the influence of the others. ->MIXED TEMPERAMENTS<-

The experience of the older <term>phrenologists</term> <note>Phrenology was a popular 19th-century field of study based on the belief that a person's character and mental abilities were determined by the shape of their skull.</note>, which is verified by the investigations of the later authorities, was that in the majority of persons *two* of the temperaments are well developed, the third remaining comparatively undeveloped. Of the two active temperaments, *one* is usually found to be predominant, although in many the two are found to be almost equally developed. But even in the last mentioned instance one of the two seems to have been more actively called forth by the environment of the person, and may therefore be regarded as the ruling temperament. Arising from this fact we find the several classes of Mixed Temperament <note>In this system, the "Vital" temperament relates to the digestive and circulatory systems, "Motive" to the bones and muscles, and "Mental" to the brain and nervous system.</note>, known, respectively, as: the Vital-Motive; the Motive-Vital; the Motive-Mental; the Mental-Motive; the Vital-Mental; and the Mental-Vital <note>In these compound terms, the first word indicates the most dominant trait, while the second indicates the secondary influence.</note>. ### THE TEMPERAMENTS

dominant, or most active temperament appears first, the second name indicating the temperament relatively undeveloped or inactive. <note>In this classification system, the first term indicates the primary influence on a person's character, while the second term indicates a secondary, supporting influence.</note>

The *Vital-Motive* and the *Motive-Vital* temperaments give the combination in which is manifested physical activity and strong vitality. Those of these temperaments are adapted to out-of-door work, such as farming, out-of-door trades, mechanics, soldiers and sailors, and other occupations requiring strong vital power and muscular strength and activity. The physical characteristics are the prominent bones and strong muscles of the <term>Motive</term> <note>The "Motive" temperament relates to the skeletal and muscular systems, associated with physical energy and endurance.</note>, and well-rounded limbs and "stout" forms of the <term>Vital</term> <note>The "Vital" temperament relates to the digestive and circulatory systems, associated with nourishment and physical robustness.</note>. When the Vital predominates, there is apt to be more flesh; when the Motive predominates there is apt to be more ruggedness and muscular development.

Those of this temperament make good practical business men, physicians, lawyers, scientists, explorers, and others who have to work and think at the same time. These people often manifest great executive ability. When the Motive predominates, the tendency is toward out-of-door occupations in which the brain is used in connection with bodily activity. When the Mental predominates there is a tendency toward in-door occupations in which active brain work is required. These people have well-developed heads, together with wiry, strong bodies. Some of the most successful men have come from this class.

# CHAPTER V

## THE MENTAL QUALITIES

We now approach the subject of the several particular **mental qualities**, and the groups thereof, both in the phase of their inner states and that of their outer form. In the consideration of both of these phases we must avail ourselves of the investigations and researches of the <term>old phrenologists</term> <note>Practitioners of a 19th-century theory—now considered a pseudoscience—which claimed that a person's character and mental capacity could be determined by the shape and bumps of their skull.</note> who cleared a path for all who follow. Although many of the phrenological theories are rejected by modern psychologists and biologists, nevertheless their work established a firm foundation for the science of the study of the brain and its functions. And to <term>Gall</term> <note>Franz Joseph Gall (1758–1828), the German neuroanatomist who founded the study of phrenology, originally calling it "cranioscopy."</note> and his followers we are indebted for the discovery and teaching that the activity and development of the several mental qualities or <term>faculties</term> <note>Distinct powers or capacities of the mind, such as memory, caution, or benevolence, which early psychologists believed were separate units of mental life.</note> manifest in outer form in the shape of the skull.

The general principles of phrenology may be briefly stated as follows:

I. The Brain is the organ of the mind.

II. The mind is not a single entity or power, but has several faculties, stronger or <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; the next page likely continues to list the "stronger or weaker" manifestations of these faculties.</note>

*   **Region of the Eye:** Form (near the bridge of the nose), Words (located directly below the eye).

As Erbes <note>Philip Erbes was an early 20th-century researcher who published "Cranio-Muscular Origins of Brain and Mind" (1904). He argued that the brain's evolution was shaped by the physical pressure and activity of muscles.</note> states it "the effect the scheme of *cranial muscles* have had and still have upon the conformation of the skull, and, consequently, had in determining the location of those areas and in giving brain and mind a character approximately identical from end to end of the scale of living things possessing the <term>cerebro-spinal nervous system</term> <note>The primary part of the nervous system consisting of the brain and spinal cord.</note>. In so far as the neural matter is dependent upon the *cranial muscles*—aside from the sensory stimuli—so far, likewise are the <term>psychic manifestations</term> <note>The outward expression of internal mental or emotional states.</note>, through tongue or limb, modified by variations in those muscles that, after their creative task is done, assume a <term>vaso-motor control</term> <note>The regulation of blood flow through the tightening or relaxing of blood vessels.</note> over their respective areas." > its character, primarily to the action of the muscles attached to and lying upon its peripheral covering, the skull; these same muscles thereafter, through exercising a cerebral vasomotor control <note>The regulation of the diameter of blood vessels, which in turn controls the amount of blood flowing to specific regions of the brain.</note>, act in the nature of keys for calling the evolved dependent brain areas into play, singly and en masse.''

Others have held that the development of certain areas of the surface of the skull is due to peculiar neural or nervous activities having their seat in certain parts of the brain adjacent to their appropriate area of the skull, but these theories fail to explain the nature of the relation between the mind, brain, and the ''nerve centres'' aforesaid.

These several authorities, and others, however, agree upon the fact that certain areas of the brain are associated in some way with certain mental states; and that these brain areas register their relative activity upon the areas of the skull adjacent thereto; and that the activity and power of each brain area, or **faculty** <note>A specific mental power or ability, such as memory, language, or caution.</note>, is denoted by the size of the associated skull-area. Thus, the outward facts claimed by <term>phrenology</term> <note>A 19th-century theory—now considered a pseudoscience—which claimed that a person's character and mental faculties could be determined by the shape and bumps of their skull.</note> are admitted, while their theories of cause are disputed.

# THE MENTAL QUALITIES

In this book we shall rest content with these "outward facts" of <term>phrenology</term> <note>A 19th-century field of study—now considered a pseudoscience—based on the belief that a person's character and mental abilities are reflected in the shape and bumps of their skull.</note>, and shall not concern ourselves with the various theories which seek to explain them, preferring to leave that task for others. In considering the subject of the **Outer Form** associated with the **Inner State of Human Nature**, we shall merely claim that mental states manifest in outer form in the shape and size of the head; and that certain areas of the skull are thus associated with certain mental states, the size and shape of the former denoting the degree of activity of the latter.

The general scheme of classification of the various mental <term>faculties</term> <note>Distinct "departments" or powers of the mind, such as memory, combativeness, or benevolence.</note> of the phrenologists, and the names given thereto by the old phrenologists, have in the main been adhered to in this book. In a number of cases, however, we have seen fit to re-arrange the groups in accordance with the later ideas of the **New Psychology** <note>A movement in the late 19th and early 20th centuries that sought to transition psychology from a branch of philosophy into an experimental and functional science.</note>, and have given to some of the "faculties" names considered more appropriate to the later classification, and understanding of the mental state. Moreover, in order to avoid the phrenological theories attaching thereto, we have decided

# CHAPTER VI
## THE EGOISTIC QUALITIES

The first group of Qualities is known as the <term>Egoistic Qualities</term> <note>In this system, "egoistic" refers to the sense of "I-ness" or personal identity, rather than meaning "selfish" in a negative way.</note>. This group is composed of two particular Qualities: **Self-Esteem** and <term>Approbativeness</term> <note>The desire for approval, praise, or a good reputation among others.</note>.

This group is physically expressed at the <term>crown</term> <note>The highest point of the back of the head, where the skull begins to curve downward.</note> of the head and on the sides directly beneath or next to the crown (See Fig. 2). It is the center for our consciousness of our own individuality and personality, as well as the behaviors that grow out of those feelings.

**Self-Esteem.** This Quality shows itself as a strong sense of personal power, self-respect, self-reliance, and dignity. It provides a sense of pride in one’s own independence and individuality. 

When this quality is found in excess, it can lead to:
*   Egotism
*   Abnormal conceit
*   <term>Imperiousness</term> <note>An arrogant or overbearing manner, as if one is a ruler over others.</note>

Conversely, a deficiency in Self-Esteem often results in a lack of self-confidence, excessive humility, and self-depreciation. This quality is what drives the ambitious spirit and the desire to hold executive positions or places of power. It strongly dislikes any assumption of au-

### THE EGOISTIC QUALITIES

crowd.'' <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "always to be found on the popular side and 'with the crowd.'"</note> They thrive upon praise, approval and notoriety, and shrink under censure, disapproval or lack of notice. One with <term>Self-Esteem</term> <note>In phrenology, the faculty of self-reliance, dignity, and independence.</note> can be happy when alone, and in fact often defies public opinion and fashion from very pride of self; while one with <term>Approbativeness</term> <note>The desire for the esteem of others; the love of praise and social standing.</note> largely developed lacks the pride to rise above approval and the opinion of others, while possessing a strong sense of vanity when public favor is bestowed. It manifests outer form at the upper-back part of the head, just above <term>Cautiousness</term> <note>The faculty associated with prudence, fear, and the instinct for security.</note> and below Self-Esteem, (see group figure) <note>Referring to the illustrative diagrams of the head used throughout phrenological texts.</note>. When largely developed it rises like two mounts on either side of Self-Esteem, but when Self-Esteem is large and Approbativeness is small, the latter appears as two sunken places on either side of Self-Esteem.

Self-Esteem values the *real self* while Approbativeness values the *appearances* of personality. The one pursues the substance, the other the shadows. Self-Esteem and Approbativeness are often confused in the minds of the public. The true keynote of the first is Pride; of the second, Vanity. The student should learn to carefully distinguish between

# CHAPTER VII

## THE MOTIVE QUALITIES

The second group is known as the <term>Selfish Qualities</term> <note>In the phrenological systems of the 19th century, "selfish" was a technical classification for instincts related to self-preservation and personal interest, rather than a purely negative moral judgment.</note>, and is composed of the following particular Qualities: *Combativeness; Destructiveness; Cunning; Cautiousness; Acquisitiveness* and *Constructiveness*. This group manifests in outer form extending along the sides of the lower head from the back toward the temples. (See Fig. 3.) <note>Phrenology textbooks typically included a "map" of the skull; Fig. 3 would have shown the locations of these faculties above the ears and toward the back of the head.</note>

COMBATIVENESS. <note>The faculty associated with courage, resistance, and the impulse to overcome opposition.</note> This Quality manifests in a strong desire to oppose, resist, combat, defy, defend. Those in whom it is developed enjoy a "scrap," <note>A colloquial term for a fight or physical argument.</note> and, in the words of the familiar saying, would "rather fight than eat." When combined with <term>Vitativeness</term> <note>The phrenological term for the "love of life" or the tenacity to survive against all odds.</note> it manifests in the tendency to fight hard for life. When combined with <term>Acquisitiveness</term> <note>The impulse to acquire property, money, or goods.</note> it manifests in the tendency to fight for money or property. When combined with <term>Amativeness</term> <note>The faculty governing physical attraction and romantic love.</note> it manifests in the tendency to fight for mates. When combined with the family-loving Qualities it manifests in a tendency to fight for the family. In fact, its particular

<summary>This page introduces the "Selfish Qualities" within the phrenological system, specifically defining Combativeness as a driving force that supports other instincts like survival, wealth-gathering, and family protection.</summary>

### Destructiveness

This Quality, manifests in a strong desire to break precedents <note>To go against established rules or previous examples.</note>, doing things in new ways, asserting authority, extermination, severity, sternness, breaking down, crushing, “walking over,” etc. Its direction is largely governed by the other Qualities, as for instance in combination with **Acquisitiveness** <term>Acquisitiveness</term> <note>The instinct to acquire and possess wealth or property.</note>, it manifests in breaking down opposition and precedents in business; while with large conscientiousness it manifests in tearing down evil conditions, etc., and in doing the work of “reform.” It generally is accompanied with large **Combativeness** <term>Combativeness</term> <note>The impulse to resist, oppose, or defend oneself.</note>, as the two go hand-in-hand. It mani- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "manifests."</note>

**CUNNING.** This Quality shows itself as a strong desire to be clever, sly, secretive, diplomatic, deceitful, and generally "foxy." It is best illustrated by the example of the fox, an animal that combines many of these traits within itself. The coyote also shows signs of having this Quality well-developed, as do birds of the crow and blackbird family, and certain types of fish. When paired with strong **Caution**, it makes a person very secretive and "close-mouthed." With strong **Acquisitiveness** <term>Acquisitiveness</term> <note>The instinct to collect, own, and profit.</note>, it makes one sly and tricky in business. With strong **Approbativeness** <term>Approbativeness</term> <note>The desire for appreciation, praise, or high social standing.</note>, it inclines a person to tell exaggerated stories that magnify their own importance and satisfy their vanity. With a vivid **Imagination**, it leads one to draw on that creative faculty to lie simply for the love of "romancing" or storytelling. It manifests its outer form a short distance above the top of the ear, immediately above **Destructiveness**, and behind **Acquisitiveness** (see the group illustration).

## CHAPTER VIII

### THE VITATIVE QUALITIES

The third group is known as the <term>Vitative Qualities</term> <note>In phrenology, these are the base instincts related to physical survival and the maintenance of life.</note>, which is composed of the three respective particular Qualities: *<term>Vitativeness</term>; <term>Alimentativeness</term>;* <note>The faculty governing the appetite for food and the sense of hunger.</note> and *<term>Bibativeness</term>.* <note>The faculty governing the desire for drink or liquids.</note> This group manifests in outer form directly back of, and in front of, the middle part of the ear. (See Fig. 4.)

**VITATIVENESS.** This quality manifests in a strong desire to live; resistance to disease and death; an intense clinging to life for the mere fact of living, rather than for the sake of anything to be accomplished by continued existence. It goes along with **Combativeness** <note>The faculty of courage, resistance, and the impulse to overcome opposition.</note>, and is especially noticeable in the "broad-headed" people and animals <note>19th-century phrenologists believed that a wider skull indicated stronger "animal" instincts and survival drives.</note>. The cat tribe, hawks, turtles, sharks, venomous snakes, and others have this <term>propensity</term> <note>A natural inclination or tendency toward a specific behavior—in this context, a survival instinct.</note> well developed, while it is deficient in the "narrow-headed" animals, such as the rabbit, certain birds, certain fish, and many harmless snakes. Those in whom it is developed "die hard," while those in whom it is deficient die easily. This

# CHAPTER IX

## THE EMOTIVE QUALITIES

The fourth group is that known as the <term>Social Qualities</term> <note>In the 19th-century system of phrenology, mental faculties were organized into groups; the Social Qualities—also called the "Domestic Propensities"—govern how individuals form bonds with family, partners, and society.</note>, which group is composed of the following particular Qualities: *Amativeness*; *Conjugality*; *Parental Love*; *Sociability and Home Love*. This group manifests outer form at the lower-back portion of the head (see Fig. 5), and shows itself by an enlargement of that region, causing the head to "bulge" back of the ears. It may best be understood by an examination of its several particular Qualities.

**AMATIVENESS.** This Quality manifests in a strong desire for sexual indulgence and association with the opposite sex. Its purpose is, of course, the reproduction of the race, but its abuse and perversion has led man to many excesses and unnatural practices. It is a dynamic propensity and its normal development is seemingly necessary in order to produce the <term>"life spirit,"</term> <note>Phrenologists believed that the "amativeness" organ acted as a battery for the body, providing a "vital spark" or general physical vigor beyond just sexual desire.</note> and vital activity mental and physical. Those in whom it is deficient lack "spirit" and energy, while those

**Vocabulary**
| Term | Definition |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Social Qualities** | The cluster of instincts regarding relationships and domestic life. |
| **Amativeness** | The faculty governing sexual attraction and procreative desire. |
| **Conjugality** | The instinct for pairing with a single mate; the drive for marriage. |
| **Parental Love** | The impulse to care for children and the helpless (Philoprogenitiveness). |
| **Sociability** | The desire for companionship and general social interaction. |
| **Home Love** | An attachment to a specific place of residence or one's country. |
| **Phrenology** | The historical study of the shape and size of the cranium as a supposed indication of character and mental abilities. |

# CHAPTER IX

## THE EMOTIVE QUALITIES

The fourth group is that known as the <term>Social Qualities</term> <note>In the 19th-century system of phrenology, mental faculties were organized into groups; the Social Qualities—also called the "Domestic Propensities"—govern how individuals form bonds with family, partners, and society.</note>, which group is composed of the following particular Qualities: *Amativeness*; *Conjugality*; *Parental Love*; *Sociability and Home Love*. This group manifests outer form at the lower-back portion of the head (see Fig. 5), and shows itself by an enlargement of that region, causing the head to "bulge" back of the ears. It may best be understood by an examination of its several particular Qualities.

**AMATIVENESS.** This Quality manifests in a strong desire for sexual indulgence and association with the opposite sex. Its purpose is, of course, the reproduction of the race, but its abuse and perversion has led man to many excesses and unnatural practices. It is a dynamic propensity and its normal development is seemingly necessary in order to produce the <term>"life spirit,"</term> <note>Phrenologists believed that the "amativeness" organ acted as a battery for the body, providing a "vital spark" or general physical vigor beyond just sexual desire.</note> and vital activity mental and physical. Those in whom it is deficient lack "spirit" and energy, while those in whom it is excessive are often dominated by their lower nature.

| **Conjugality** | The instinct for pairing with a single mate; the drive for marriage. |
| **Parental Love** | Th... <note>Text truncated in original; likely "The love for offspring or children."</note> |

**CONJUGALITY.** <note>In phrenology, this faculty represents the instinct for monogamy and the desire for a permanent, exclusive partnership.</note> This quality manifests in a strong desire for a “mate”—and *one mate* only. While **Amativeness** <note>The faculty of physical love and sexual attraction.</note> may cause one to seek the society of many of the opposite sex, **Conjugality** will act only to cause one to seek the one life partner. **Conjugality** causes the desire to “mate for life.” It is something quite different from **Amativeness**, although of course related to it. The location of its outer form, between **Amativeness** and **Friendship**, gives the key to its quality—love with companionship. Those in whom it is well developed are very close to their mates and tend toward jealousy; they suffer intensely when the relation is inharmonious or disturbed in any way, and are often broken-hearted at disappointment in love or the death of the mate. Those in whom it is deficient feel very little true companionship for their mates, and with **Amativeness** large are apt to be promiscuous in their manifestation of love or passion; if one love is interrupted

### PARENTAL LOVE.
This Quality manifests in a strong desire for and love of children, particularly one’s own. Those in whom it is very strong often adopt children in addition to their own and love to caress children wherever and whenever they may see them. It manifests its outer form at the lower-back part of the head on the middle-line of the head, above <term>Amativeness</term>, and below <term>Inhabitiveness</term> <note>The faculty representing the love of home, country, and the desire for a fixed place of residence.</note> (see group figure).

### SOCIABILITY.
This Quality manifests in a strong desire for companionship, fellowship, friends, sympathy, society, associates, etc. It is the “social sense.” Those in whom it is strong feel happy only when surrounded by associates, friends, or boon companions.

### HOME-LOVE.
This Quality manifests in a strong love of familiar places, particularly of one’s home and near-by country, and from this springs love of country and patriotism. Those in whom it is strong dislike to travel, and are subject to home-sickness. Those in whom it is weak are fond of travel, readily change their places of abode, and are apt to become “roamers” if they indulge the Quality. ### CHAPTER X
### THE APPLICATIVE QUALITIES

The fifth group, known as the <term>Applicative Qualities</term> <note>In phrenological terms, these are traits that determine how a person applies their mental energy and focus to their goals.</note>, is composed of two particular Qualities, known, respectively, as *Firmness* and *Continuity*. This group manifests in outer form on the centre-line of the head, just above and just below the "crown," at which latter point <term>Self-Esteem</term> <note>The faculty governing self-respect, independence, and personal dignity, located at the highest point of the back of the head.</note> is situated (see Fig. 6).

**FIRMNESS.** This Quality manifests in a strong tendency toward stability, tenacity, fixedness of purpose, and decision. When very highly developed with the reasoning powers weak it often manifests as stubbornness, mulishness, obstinacy, etc. Those in whom it is largely developed display firmness in decision, are "set in their ways," cannot be driven by force or converted by argument when they have once formed an opinion and taken a stand. The "indomitable will" arises from this Quality; in fact, this Quality might well be termed the "Will Quality," although it manifests by that as—

<summary>This page introduces the Applicative Qualities, focusing on "Firmness" as the primary driver of willpower and persistence. It explains that while Firmness provides stability and resolve, it can devolve into stubbornness if the individual lacks strong reasoning skills.</summary>

It is an indispensable prerequisite of greatness and goodness. Without it great talents are of little avail, for they accomplish little; but with it large, fair to middling capacities accomplish commendable results. ## CHAPTER XI

### THE MODIFICATIVE QUALITIES

The sixth group is known as the <term>Modificative Qualities</term> <note>In phrenological theory, these are faculties that "modify" or refine the expression of other traits, adding a layer of polish or sophistication to a person's character.</note> (called by the <term>phrenologists</term> <note>Followers of the 19th-century study of "reading" the bumps on the human skull to determine character and mental capacity.</note> "The <term>Self-Perfecting Group</term>" <note>This group was believed to be responsible for self-improvement and the elevation of the human mind above mere animal instinct.</note>), which is composed of the following particular Qualities: *Ideality, Infinity,* and *Humor,* respectively. This group manifests outer form in the region of the temples, and when large gives width to the sides of the fore part of the head (See Figure 7).

**IDEALITY.** This Quality <note>The faculty associated with the love of the beautiful, the imaginative, and the perfect.</note> could well be called the "Artistic" quality of the mind. It manifests in a strong desire for the beautiful, the ideal, the elegant, the polished, the graceful, the refined. It is also closely connected with the phase of mental activity called "Imagination." Those in whom it is largely developed manifest the artistic taste and temperament, the love of art, beauty and the ideal, the poetic spirit, the love of the refined and choice—and a corresponding dislike for all opposed to these tastes and qualities.

<summary>This page introduces the "Modificative Qualities," a phrenological group focused on self-perfection and refinement, specifically defining "Ideality" as the seat of artistic taste and imagination.</summary>
<keywords>phrenology, Modificative Qualities, Ideality, Self-Perfecting Group, artistic temperament, imagination, aesthetics</keywords>

**IDEALITY.** This Quality <note>The faculty associated with the love of the beautiful, the imaginative, and the perfect.</note> could well be called the "Artistic" quality of the mind. It manifests in a strong desire for the beautiful, the ideal, the elegant, the polished, the graceful, the refined. It is also closely connected with the phase of mental activity called "Imagination." Those in whom it is largely developed manifest the artistic taste and temperament, the love of art, beauty and the ideal, the poetic spirit, the love of the refined and choice—and a corresponding dislike for all opposed to these tastes and qualities.

In addition to the above characteristics, which are largely due to the co-operation of **Mysticism**, **Infinity**, and **Reverence**, there is another set of manifestations which were largely overlooked by the older <term>phrenologists</term> <note>Followers of a 19th-century theory that specific mental "faculties" were located in distinct areas of the brain, identifiable by the shape of the skull.</note>—the activity of the **Imagination** in connection with **Constructiveness**. This combination of **Constructiveness** and **Ideality** is found in the great scientists, inventors, great financiers, and others whose plans for “building up” show that **Ideality** has been also very active in the direction of picturing “what may be”—the ideal which **Construction** makes real. In much mental constructive work, there is found the artistic element, which arises from **Ideality**. This Quality manifests outer form in the upper and frontal portion of the temples, just where the head begins to curve upward, and just in front of, or under, the edges of the hair (see group figure). It is just above **Constructiveness**, and just below **Mysticism** and **Imitation**, a position which throws light on its several phases of manifestation above noted.

<note>The following terms refer to the specific "organs" or mental faculties discussed in the text:
- **Mysticism**: The faculty of faith, spirituality, and the supernatural.
- **Infinity**: A sense of vastness or the eternal.
- **Reverence**: The feeling of respect or awe for what is great or sacred.
- **Phrenologists**: 19th-century researchers who mapped personality traits to the shape of the head.
- **Imagination**: The faculty of forming mental images of things not present to the senses.
- **Constructiveness**: The desire and ability to build, invent, or organize physically or mentally.
- **Ideality**: The love of beauty, perfection, and refinement.
- **Construction**: The act of making the "ideal" vision into a reality.
- **Imitation**: The ability to copy, mimic, or learn from the behavior of others.</note>

### INFINITY

This Quality <note>In other phrenological texts, this faculty is often referred to as "Sublimity."</note> manifests in a strong realization of the grand, the majestic, the vast, the illimitable, the infinite, the eternal, the absolute, the omnipotent <note>all-powerful</note>, the omnipresent <note>present everywhere</note>, the omniscient <note>all-knowing</note>. It is the realizing sense of **The Great**. Those in whom it is large are impressed by the sublime, the majestic, the grand, in nature or in thought and conception. **Niagara** <note>A reference to Niagara Falls, which was a standard 19th-century example of the "sublime" in nature.</note>; the great work of the architect; the thunder-storm; the giant redwood of **California**; the ocean; or the thoughts of **Infinity**, alike appeal to the one in whom this Quality is large. 

If **Reverence** <note>The faculty associated with respect, awe, and religious devotion.</note> be large, the trend of **Infinity** will be toward religious ideas—the greatness of God. If the intellectual faculties be in the ascendency, **Infinity** will lead to high conceptions of Space, Nature, the Infinite. If **Ideality** <note>The faculty governing the appreciation of beauty, refinement, and perfection.</note> be large, **Infinity** will incline toward the grand and great in art. If **Constructiveness** <note>The faculty related to manual skill, building, and the desire to organize or invent.</note> be well developed, **Infinity** will impel to the creation of great works, enterprises, buildings, schemes, or what not. **Infinity** influences everything in the direction of largeness and greatness. # CHAPTER XII
## THE RELATIVE QUALITIES

The seventh group is known as the Relative Qualities <note>In phrenology, these are the "social" faculties that govern how an individual interacts with and perceives other people.</note>, and is composed of the following four particular Qualities: *Human Nature*; *Suavity*; *Sympathy*; and *Imitation*; respectively. The designation “Relative” is applied to this group, by reason of the fact that its activities are concerned with the relations between the individual and others of his kind. The group manifests outer form in the front-upper part of the head, beginning just above the line of the hair, from which it extends backward toward the top-head. (See Fig. 8.) <note>Fig. 8 likely refers to a diagram in the original text showing the mapping of these traits on the skull.</note>

HUMAN NATURE. This Quality manifests in a strong desire to read character, discern human motives, interpret feelings and thoughts, and to *know* men and women thoroughly. Those in whom it is large seem to read the mind, motives and character of those whom they meet, in an almost intuitive manner—the ideas, feelings, thoughts, motives and designs of others seem like an open book to them. They are natural <term>physiognomists</term> <note>Practitioners of physiognomy, the historical study of judging a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note>, and under-

<page-num>116</page-num>

### HUMAN NATURE

stand <note>Continuing the sentence from page 115: "...and understand..."</note> **Human Nature** in both its inner states and outer forms. This quality is largely developed in successful **salesmen**, **detectives**, **credit-men** <note>A historical term for professionals who assessed the financial reliability and character of potential borrowers.</note>, **politicians**, and others whose success depends largely upon the ability to read the character of those with whom they come in contact. This Quality concerns itself with the entire subject matter of this book, and is of the utmost importance to every individual. It should be developed and trained.

**Prof. O. S. Fowler** <note>Orson Squire Fowler (1809–1887) was a famed American phrenologist and author who believed that human character could be improved through self-study and physical health.</note> explains its manifestations, and at the same time directs one along the lines of its cultivation, as follows:

> "Scan closely all the actions of men, in order to ascertain their motives and mainsprings of action; look with a sharp eye at man, woman and child, all you meet, as if you would read them through; note particularly the expression of the eye, as if you would imbibe what it signifies; say to yourself, what faculty prompted this expression and that action? drink in the general looks, attitude, natural language and manifestations of men, and yield yourself to the impressions naturally made on you; that is, study human nature both as a **philosophy** and a **sentiment** <note>In this context, "sentiment" refers to an intuitive or emotional feeling about a person's character, as opposed to a purely logical analysis.</note>."

This Quality <note>Referring to the faculty of "Human Nature" discussed on the previous page.</note> manifests in outer form on the middle-line of the summit of the forehead, just where the hair usually begins to appear, and from thence slightly upward around the curve (see group figure). It is directly above **Analysis** <note>In phrenology, the faculty used to break down complex ideas into simpler parts.</note> and is often mistaken for a continuation thereof. Its nearness to that Quality indicates its relationship thereto, the connection being very close; in fact, some authorities have treated it as a particular phase of **Analysis**. It is directly in front of and below **Sympathy**, which position is also suggestive, for we must first understand the feelings of others before we can sympathize with them. It is between the two lobes of **Suavity** <note>The faculty of being pleasant and agreeable; also known as "Agreeableness."</note>, which position is also suggestive, for **Suavity** depends upon an understanding of the character and feelings of others, in order that we may “fall in” with the same. In the same way **Imitation**, which closely adjoins it, depends upon **Human Nature** for its copying material. When largely developed this Quality gives a peculiar fullness and height to the upper forehead.

# CHAPTER XIII

## THE PERCEPTIVE QUALITIES

The eighth group is known as the **Perceptive Qualities**, composed of the following particular Qualities, respectively: *Observation*; *Form*; *Size*; *Weight*; *Color*; *Order*; *Calculation*; *Tune*; *Time*; *Locality*; <term>Eventuality</term> <note>Eventuality: In phrenology, this refers to the faculty for remembering facts, events, and historical details—the "memory of stories."</note>, and *Words*. This group manifests outer form in the lower part of the forehead, in the region of the eye. (See Fig. 9.) <note>Figure 9, though not pictured here, would have shown a diagram of the skull with these specific regions mapped out near the brow bone.</note> When large, this group often gives to the upper forehead the appearance of "retreating" or sloping backward. <term>Prof. O. S. Fowler</term> <note>Orson Squire Fowler (1809–1887) was a leading American phrenologist who wrote extensively on how physical traits reveal personality.</note> says of the appearance of those Qualities which manifest outer form under the eyebrows: 

> "The following rule for observing their size obviates the objection sometimes urged that the eyebrows and their arches prevent the correct diagnosis of these smaller <term>organs</term> <note>In phrenology, "organs" refer to specific areas of the brain believed to correspond to different mental faculties.</note> crowded so thickly together. The rule is: *The shape of the eyebrows reveals the size, absolute and relative, of each, thus: When all are large, the eyebrow is long and arching; when all are deficient, it is short and straight; when some are* <note>The text ends mid-sentence and continues on the following page.</note>

### EVENTUALITY

This <term>Quality</term> <note>In phrenology, a "Quality" or "Faculty" refers to a specific mental power housed in a distinct part of the brain.</note> manifests in a cognizance, appreciation, and recollection of facts, events, happenings, occurrences, *news*, etc. Those in whom it is large most readily perceive, recognize, and remember striking events, facts, doings, occurrences—in short, *news*. Such persons have the "nose for news" which is so important to the news-paper man, scientific investigator, researcher in any line, and general investigator. It is the "historical faculty," and the "journalistic sense," as well as an important part of the "scientific instinct." These people make good witnesses, story tellers, and entertainers. They know "what is going on," and are the people to go to when one wishes to "hear the news," or to learn the past history of anything or anybody.

This Quality manifests its outer form in the centre of the forehead, immediately above <term>Observation</term> <note>Also known as 'Individuality,' this is the faculty for noticing and identifying individual objects.</note>, and in front of <term>Locality</term> <note>The faculty responsible for remembering places and spatial relationships.</note> (see group figure). When large it tends to "fill out" the middle of the forehead. **Prof. O. S. Fowler** <note>Orson Squire Fowler (1809–1887) was a pioneering American phrenologist who helped turn the practice into a massive cultural movement in the 19th century.</note> says: "It sometimes seems deficient,

**WORDS.** This <term>Quality</term> <note>In the system of phrenology, a "Quality" or "Faculty" is a specific mental power or instinct supposed to be located in a particular "organ" or area of the brain.</note> manifests in a cognizance <note>A formal term for awareness, notice, or knowledge.</note>, appreciation and recollection of words, *terms, phrases, etc.,* and their meanings. to receive verbal Impressions and to manifest verbal Expression. It produces the orator, and the adept in the use of words in writing. To those persons in whom it is largely developed, words take on life and reality, and become living thought. In excess, it produces verbosity, talkativeness, and "windiness" <note>A 19th-century term for being "long-winded" or boastful without much substance.</note> of expression. When deficient, it renders one unable to properly express himself. It manifests outer form above and partly behind the <term>superorbital plates</term> <note>The bony plates of the frontal bone that form the ceiling of the eye sockets.</note>, which form the roof of the sockets of the eyes, and when large tends to press the eyes forward and downward. Its location was discovered by <term>Gall</term> <note>Franz Joseph Gall (1758–1828), the German physician who founded the study of phrenology.</note>, who observed that those fluent in the use of words almost always had full and prominent eyes, and prominent under eye-lids. The fullness of the eyes and lower eyelids, therefore, is its distinguishing mark.

Professor <term>O. S. Fowler</term> <note>Orson Squire Fowler (1809–1887), a famous American phrenologist and publisher who popularized the practice in the United States.</note> says: "See how the eyes stand out beyond the cheekbone—the best standard points from which to estimate its size, because, though it may be large, yet the <term>Perceptives</term> <note>In phrenology, the "Perceptive Qualities" or "Perceptives" are a group of faculties located in the lower forehead responsible for observing physical objects and their properties.</note> may be still larger, in which case the latter will project forward still farther even beyond large <term>Expression</term>."
