(Words). Hence the fullness of the eyes should not be compared with the eyebrows as much as with the bone below them <note>The cheekbone, which provides a stable anatomical reference point compared to the more variable brow ridge.</note>, which not being subject to kindred <term>mutations</term> <note>In this context, "mutations" refers to the anatomical variations or changes in bone shape that occur between different individuals.</note>, forms a correct measuring point of observation." It may be objected to that <term>Quality of Words</term> <note>The mental faculty associated with the command of language and verbal memory.</note> is not, strictly speaking, a <term>Perceptive</term> <note>A category of phrenological faculties located in the lower forehead used for observing and classifying the physical world.</note>, but when it is realized that before words may be fluently used, they must be perceived, recognized, and remembered, the reason for our inclusion of this Quality in the Perceptive class may be understood.

**Vocabulary**
*   **Human Nature:** The study of character and personality through physical traits.
*   **Fullness of the eyes:** A physical protrusion of the eyeball or eyelids, thought by phrenologists to indicate linguistic talent.
*   **Quality of Words:** The specific mental power governing speech and vocabulary.
*   **Perceptive class:** The group of mental faculties responsible for taking in information through the senses.
*   **Mutations:** Structural variations in the skull or face.
*   **Observation:** The practice of reading character through physical signs.

## CHAPTER XIV

### THE REFLECTIVE QUALITIES

The ninth group is known as the Reflective Qualities, which is composed of the two following particular Qualities: *Analysis* and *Logic*, respectively. This group is accorded the highest place among the mental Qualities, for Reason is ranked higher than Emotion, Feeling or Sentiment. <margin>The Reflective Qualities are positioned at the very top of the intellectual faculties.</margin> Its purpose is to philosophize, penetrate, investigate, originate, pursue the processes of <term>inductive</term> <note>A method of reasoning that moves from specific observations to broader generalizations.</note> and <term>deductive reasoning</term> <note>A method of reasoning that starts with a general statement or hypothesis and examines the possibilities to reach a specific, logical conclusion.</note>, analyze, <term>synthesize</term> <note>The act of combining different ideas or elements to form a connected whole.</note>, take apart, put together, combine, harmonize, search for, discover, and to manifest all the processes of Rational Thought, using the report of the <term>Perceptives</term> <note>The faculties discussed in previous chapters that gather raw sensory data from the outside world.</note> as "raw material." 

This group manifests outer form in the upper part of the forehead, immediately above the Perceptive Qualities. (See Fig. 10.) When large it gives to the upper part of the forehead that appearance of intellectuality, which is so commonly recognized, and which has given rise to the semi-slang phrase <term>"high-brow"</term> <note>A term that originated in phrenology; it suggests that a person with a tall or "high" forehead possesses superior intelligence or refined taste.</note> as applied to persons manifesting intellect.

<summary>This page introduces the "Reflective Qualities"—Analysis and Logic—which represent the highest functions of the human mind, located in the upper forehead. It explains how these faculties use sensory data to perform complex tasks like philosophizing, synthesizing ideas, and logical reasoning.</summary>
<keywords>Reflective Qualities, Analysis, Logic, Reason, Inductive Reasoning, Deductive Reasoning, High-brow, Phrenology, Rational Thought</keywords>

->THE REFLECTIVE QUALITIES<-

**ANALYSIS.** This Quality manifests in a strong desire to <term>analyze</term> <note>To break down a complex topic or substance into smaller parts to better understand it.</note>, compare, classify, infer, discriminate, illustrate, etc. It gathers together the "raw material" of perception, and proceeds to analyze and compare its particular parts, and then to group the parts together in a new classification and <term>synthesis</term> <note>The combination of ideas to form a theory or system; the opposite of analysis.</note>. Those in whom it is largely developed manifest the power of comparison to a high degree, discovering points of resemblance and difference almost intuitively. They will plunge to the heart of a subject in a short time, and will be able to extract the essence of an object or subject with comparatively little effort.

Spurzheim <note>Johann Gaspar Spurzheim (1776–1832), a German physician and one of the primary founders of phrenology.</note> says of it: "The great law of this faculty seems to be to form abstract ideas, generalizations, and harmony among the operations of the other faculties. . . . It pre-supposes, however, the activity of the other faculties, and cannot act upon them if they are inactive."

Professor Nelson Sizer <note>Nelson Sizer (1812–1897) was a prominent American phrenologist who specialized in vocational guidance.</note> says that it, "frequently discovers unexpected resemblances among other things, and people who have it in a very active condition are constantly surprising those in whom it is dull by their novel..."

It endeavors to prove that one thing is of such and such a nature, because it resembles another that is so and so; and because the majority of people have it fairly developed, they are prone to convert an illustration into an argument. It exercises a most important influence upon the mind in the way of analytical capability; and one who has it largely developed is quick in discovering and understanding differences, enigmatical <note>Difficult to interpret or understand; mysterious.</note> assertions and improper or inaccurate allusions; hence it is essential to critical <term>acumen</term> <note>The ability to make good judgments and quick decisions, typically in a particular domain.</note>.”

The majority of scientists have it largely developed, as also do discoverers in all lines of investigation.

### LOGIC <note>In phrenology, this faculty is more commonly labeled "Causality." It was believed to be located on the upper outer sides of the forehead.</note>

This Quality manifests in a strong desire to inquire into the "Why?" of things—into Causes—into the "Wherefore?"; and to reason therefrom to effects and application of laws. Those in whom it is large manifest the power of logical reasoning to a high degree, and abhor fallacies. This is the philosophical faculty of mind. It searches back of facts and phenomena for causes, motives and laws, and then reasons deductively <note>Deductive reasoning moves from general laws or principles to specific instances.</note> from these. Combe <note>George Combe (1788–1858), a Scottish lawyer whose book "The Constitution of Man" was a defining text for 19th-century phrenology.</note> says: "This faculty prompts us on all occasions to ask, 'Why is this so, and what is its object?' It demands reasons and proofs in the reasoning of its owner, as well as from others."

# HUMAN NATURE

This Quality <note>The faculty of "Logic," or Causality, which was the focus of the preceding page.</note> manifests its outer form on the sides of the upper part of the forehead, situated on either side of **Analysis** <note>In phrenology, this is the faculty of "Comparison," used to see similarities and differences.</note> and above **Locality** <note>The faculty associated with the memory of places and spatial relationships.</note> (see the group figure). When large, it gives the forehead a "high, bold, square" form. With large **Perceptives** <note>The "perceptive faculties" are those located just above the eyebrows, used for gathering physical data from the environment.</note>, this Quality does not present such a prominent appearance or marked contrast; however, when the **Perceptives** are small, it gives the brow an "overhanging" appearance. With **Analysis** being equally—or nearly as—strong, the fullness naturally extends well across the forehead. But when **Analysis** is much smaller, **Logic** presents as a bulging on each side of the forehead; conversely, when **Analysis** is large and **Logic** is small, the latter creates the appearance of two depressions on each side of the forehead.

Spurzheim <note>Johann Gaspar Spurzheim (1776–1832), a German physician and one of the primary founders of phrenology.</note> well says of the combination of **Analysis** and **Logic** (which he terms "Comparison" and "Causality," respectively): "Causality and Comparison combined constitute Reason. Without Causality (**Logic**) there can be no argumentative reasoning; without Comparison (**Analysis**), no comprehensive views, and no nice distinctions. Observation teaches objects, and **Eventuality** <note>The phrenological faculty responsible for the memory of facts, events, and history.</note>

## CHAPTER XV

### THE RELIGIO-MORAL QUALITIES

The tenth group is known as the **Religio-Moral Qualities** <note>In the phrenological system of this period, these qualities represent the "higher" human faculties that govern spirituality, ethics, and one's relationship with the divine and the community.</note>, and is composed of the following particular Qualities: *Reverence*, *Mysticism*, *Optimism*, and *Conscientiousness*, respectively. This group manifests its outer form at the front-top of the head, and on either side thereof (see Fig. 11).

**REVERENCE.** This Quality manifests in a strong reverence, respect and awe for and of higher beings, persons in authority, sacred things, religious ideas, constituted authority, leaders, teachers, and heroes. It may be symbolically expressed by the word, “Worship.” Like that of *Mysticism*, this Quality contains within its field the highest and the lowest. It manifests the reverence and veneration for the highest conceptions of **Deity** <note>A term for God or a supreme being.</note> and Being; and also the fear and base servile worship of idols, demoniac deities, devil-gods, etc. Likewise, it manifests in respect and submission for the lawfully constituted authorities; and also for false leaders and prophets, charla- <note>The text ends mid-word; it likely intends to say "charlatans," referring to fraudulent practitioners or deceivers.</note>

tans and imposters. In the same way it causes a hero-worship for those who have performed meritorious tasks and have wrought good for the race; but also for the unworthy persons whose sensational deeds have brought them into the "limelight" <note>A term for intense public attention, originally referring to a type of stage lighting used in theaters.</note> of notoriety. It manifests in all forms of the highest religion; and in the lowest forms of devil-worship and low superstitious awe and fear, in the richest religious experiences, and in the wildest fanaticism and hallucinations. The direction of the manifestation is decided by the relative development of the other propensities <note>In the context of phrenology, "propensities" are the natural inclinations or tendencies of the mind.</note>, particularly those of the reasoning faculties.

This Quality <note>Referring to the quality of "Reverence" discussed on the previous page.</note> manifests outer form on the middle-top of the head, along the middle-line directly in front of <term>Firmness</term> <note>The organ of persistence and willpower.</note>, back of <term>Sympathy</term>, and just above <term>Mysticism</term> and <term>Optimism</term> (see group figure). When largely developed, it causes the middle of the top of the head to "bulge," particularly if <term>Mysticism</term> be also largely developed, the combination usually being thus.

**MYSTICISM.** This Quality manifests in a strong attraction for the supernatural, the marvellous, the unknown, the mysterious.

<summary>This page describes how the quality of "Reverence" can result in either noble worship or fanatical superstition depending on a person's reason, and provides its specific physical location on the head. It also introduces "Mysticism" as the drive toward the supernatural.</summary>

<keywords>phrenology, Reverence, Mysticism, hero-worship, superstition, reasoning faculties, Firmness, Sympathy, cranial mapping</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Mysticism" within the section on Religio-Moral Qualities. It transitions from the previous page's introduction of the term to its practical manifestations in character and behavior.</meta>

### THE RELIGIO-MORAL QUALITIES

When perverted <note>Meaning when this quality is over-developed or misused without the guidance of reason.</note> it leads to superstition, gross credulity <note>An excessive readiness to believe things without proper evidence.</note>, belief in witchcraft; faith in signs, omens, and warnings, etc. When balanced by certain other Qualities it leads one to the higher flights of religious experience, faith, and consciousness of the "light within;" <note>A spiritual concept referring to an inner divine presence or intuitive guidance, often associated with the Quakers.</note> but when not so balanced it leads one to credulity, superstition and religious, occult, and mystical imposture.

"Psychic" phenomena are familiar to those in whom it is largely developed in connection with certain other mental qualities; clairvoyance, second-sight, spirit-vision and other peculiar experiences being common to these people. The prophets, seers, and wonder-workers belong to this class of "psychics." Poets possess this Quality in many cases. The manifestations of this Quality include some of the very highest and the very lowest of "spiritual" experiences and feelings. This paradox is explained when we consider the influence of the other Qualities, high and low, operating in connection with that of <term>Mysticism</term> <note>In the context of this book, Mysticism refers to the specific mental faculty or "organ" that senses the supernatural.</note>. In the garden of Mysticism grow the choicest flowers and the rankest and most noxious weeds.

**Religio-Moral Qualities**, **superstition**, **witchcraft**, **light within**, **occult**, **psychic**, **clairvoyance**, **second-sight**, **spirit-vision**, **prophets**, **seers**, **wonder-workers**, **Mysticism**

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of the "Mysticism" faculty, explaining how it can lead to either profound spiritual enlightenment or harmful superstition and fraud. It identifies this quality as the source of psychic abilities and the visionary inspiration found in prophets and poets.</summary>
<keywords>Mysticism, phrenology, superstition, psychic phenomena, clairvoyance, religious experience, spiritualism, Religio-Moral Qualities</keywords>

## HUMAN NATURE

This Quality <note>In the system of phrenology, the faculty of "Human Nature" is believed to give a person the ability to intuitively discern the character, motives, and intentions of others through observation.</note> is located immediately in front of Optimism, and below on either side of Reverence, on the front-upper part of the head (see group figure). When developed it renders the front top-head broad and prominent.

**OPTIMISM.** This Quality manifests in a strong tendency to look on the bright side of things, to expect the best, to anticipate the best. Spurzheim <note>Johann Gaspar Spurzheim (1776–1832) was a German physician and one of the founding fathers of phrenology. He was responsible for popularizing the system in the English-speaking world.</note> says of it: 

> “Hope is necessary to the happiness of man in almost all situations and often gives more satisfaction than even success. Those who are everlastingly scheming or building castles in the air have it large. It believes possible whatever the other faculties desire. It is not confined to this life, but inspires hopes of a future state, and belief in the immortality of the soul. When too strong it expects the unreasonable and impossible; but when too weak, with <term>Caution</term> <note>In phrenology, "Caution" (or Cautiousness) is the organ located toward the back-side of the head associated with prudence, anxiety, and the instinct to avoid danger.</note> large, it produces low spirits, melancholy and despair.”

This Quality when full produces optimists; when weak, pessimists; when medium, the average person who swings between the two extremes partaking of the nature of each.

<summary>This page describes the phrenological faculties of Human Nature and Optimism, explaining their physical locations on the skull and their influence on personality and outlook.</summary>
<keywords>Human Nature, Optimism, Spurzheim, Reverence, Caution, Phrenology, skull mapping, personality traits</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Optimism" (also referred to by phrenologists as "Hope") and transitions into the definition and location of "Conscientiousness," both of which fall under the category of the Religio-Moral Qualities.</meta>

->THE RELIGIO-MORAL QUALITIES<-

Those in whom it <note>Referring to the faculty of Optimism or "Hope" discussed on the previous page.</note> is developed to excess are apt to see success in everything, and with a lively imagination translate dreams into realities; of these persons it has been said: “show them an egg, and the next minute the air is full of feathers.” When this Quality is weak the person is disposed to look for the worm in the apple, decay at the heart of the rose, and for the skeleton beneath the form of beauty. It has been said that “the optimist sees nothing but the body of the doughnut; the pessimist, nothing but the hole.” <note>This well-known adage is used here to illustrate how phrenological "organs" were thought to dictate a person's entire worldview.</note>

This <term>Propensity</term> <note>In the context of phrenology, a "propensity" refers to a specific mental inclination or "organ" located in a particular area of the brain/skull.</note> manifests outer form at the middle sides of the upper head, in front of Conscientiousness, back of <term>Spirituality</term> <note>Spirituality, in this system, is the faculty associated with faith, wonder, and the belief in the unseen or supernatural.</note> (see group figure).

**CONSCIENTIOUSNESS.** This Quality manifests in a strong tendency to act according to truth, principle, duty, the accepted code of ethics, conception of right, accepted religious teachings—in short to regulate conduct according to the particular standard of “right and wrong” accepted by the person. Those in whom it is large feel keenly their personal responsibility, duty, and moral obligation. With <term>Reverence</term> <note>Reverence is the phrenological organ associated with respect for hierarchy, antiquity, and the divine.</note> large, they model their stand-

In fact the Quality itself gives rise to what is generally called the "social conscience."

<term>Combe</term> <note>George Combe (1788–1858) was a Scottish lawyer and a leader of the phrenological movement; his book "The Constitution of Man" was a Victorian bestseller.</note> says of this <term>Propensity</term>: 
> "After more than thirty years experience of the world in actual life, and in various countries, I cannot remember an instance in which I have been permanently treated unjustly by one in whom this organ and intellect were large. Momentary injustice, through irritation or misrepresentation, may have been done; but after correct information and time to become cool, I have found such persons ever disposed to act on the dictates of Conscience; as well satisfied with justice. . . . It leads to punctuality in keeping appointments so as not to waste their time; to the ready payment of debts; will not send collectors away unsatisfied except from inability to pay; are reserved in making promises, but punctual in keeping them; and when favorably combined, are consistent in conduct. . . .

## THE RELIGIO-MORAL QUALITIES

In normal manifestation this Quality <note>Referring to the phrenological faculty of Conscientiousness.</note> renders its possessor a most worthy and estimable individual; but when abnormally developed and not balanced by judgment and the reasoning faculties, it produces persecutors and religious and ethical tyrants, adhering to the letter of the law rather than to its spirit. <term>Conscience</term> <note>In this context, the author refers to the phrenological organ of Conscientiousness, which governs the sense of right and wrong.</note> is generally esteemed, but careful observers deplore the "ingrown conscience" and "blue-law spirit" <note>A reference to strict, puritanical laws designed to enforce morality, particularly those that restricted activity on Sundays.</note> of those of large <term>Conscientiousness</term>, large <term>Destructiveness</term> <note>The organ associated with force, severity, and the impulse to break down or destroy.</note>, and small <term>Sympathy</term> <note>Also called "Benevolence"; the organ of compassion and altruistic concern for others.</note>. Many so-called "reformers" belong to this last class. ## CHAPTER XVI

### FACES

Next to the shape of the head, the facial expression furnishes us with the most marked indication of the outer form accompanying the inner mental state. <note>The author is referring to the core 19th-century belief that a person’s thoughts and character physically "carve" themselves into their physical appearance.</note> In fact, many authorities hold that the facial expression affords the most easily read and most comprehensive index of character, and that, therefore, <term>Physiognomy</term> <note>The practice of judging character or personality from facial features.</note> possesses many points of superiority over <term>Phrenology</term> <note>The study of the shape and protuberances of the skull as an indicator of mental faculties.</note>. The truth seems to be that <term>Physiognomy</term> and <term>Phrenology</term> are twin-sciences, and that the true student of Human Nature should acquaint himself thoroughly with both.

<term>Physiognomy</term> is "the science and art of discovering or reading the temper and other characteristic qualities of the mind by the features of the face." The philosophy underlying the science of <term>Physiognomy</term> has been stated at length in the first several chapters of this book, the essence of which is that *mental states manifest in outward form*. The majority of persons apply the principles of

<summary>This page introduces Physiognomy as a vital companion to Phrenology, arguing that facial expressions are a direct and readable map of an individual's internal character.</summary>
<keywords>Physiognomy, Phrenology, facial expression, character reading, mental states, human nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 156, which argued that most people instinctively use the principles of character reading in their daily interactions. It also introduces the observations of Nelson Sizer, a prominent figure in the study of human nature during the 1800s.</meta>

# FACES

<term>Physiognomy</term> <note>The practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note> more or less unconsciously in judging the characters of those with whom they come in contact. Nearly every one scans closely the features of those whom they meet for the first time, and form a general impression therefrom. Children and domestic animals possess an instinctive knowledge of facial expression and can often tell very accurately the general disposition toward them possessed by various persons. Certain persons are generally considered to "look stupid," while others have "a bright, intelligent expression"; some look "tricky," while others "look honest" and trustworthy.

Professor <insert>Nelson Sizer</insert> <note>Nelson Sizer (1812–1897) was a leading American phrenologist and author who served as the president of the American Institute of Phrenology.</note> says: 

> "Though all human beings have the general human form and features—though all have eyes, nose, mouth, chin, etc., yet each one has a different face and look from every other. And, more, yet, the same person has a very different facial look at different times, according as he is angry or friendly, etc. And always the same look when in the same mood. Of course, then, something causes this expression—especially, since all who are angry, friendly, etc., have one general or similar expression; that"

Nor are they under the control of the will, for they act spontaneously. We cannot help, whether we will or no, laughing when merry, even though in church, pouting when provoked, and expressing all our mental operations, down even to the very innermost recesses of our souls, in and by our countenances. <meta>This page continues the quote from Professor Henry S. Drayton regarding the physical manifestation of character and emotion, concluding the discussion on how internal states become habitual physical traits.</meta>

# Faces

exist in a given individual. . . . Each of the <term>mental organs</term><note>In phrenology, the "organs" were specific areas of the brain believed to be responsible for different personality traits, such as kindness or combativeness.</note> has its natural language, as shown in **pantomime**, which is exhibited by the gestures and motions of the head, hands, and body. Children and animals read the feelings of their parents or masters by their motions and attitudes, which are often more influential than words. 

The brain is the central source of motive and mental power; every action has its root or seat of impulse in the brain and its connections. As the mind forms purposes, the will is sent out to the extremities, and the external motions express the inward thought and feeling. 

Habitual states of mind tend to produce habitual forms and expressions of face and body; a person who suffers pain for years will have in the face an expression of that internal state. One who has been nurtured in gladness, though the face may not be beautiful, will wear the "sunshine of joy." One who has had care and responsibility will come to show it in the face, in the walk, and in the voice, just as one who has been subjugated and kept subordinate will have the word "humiliation" written across their features.

### SHAPES OF FACES

The authorities in <term>Physiognomy</term><note>Physiognomy was a popular historical practice of assessing a person's character, personality, or ethnic origin based on their outward appearance, particularly the features of the face.</note> divide the faces of persons into three general classes, namely: (1) The Round Face; (2) The Oblong Face; and (3) The Pear-shaped Face.

**Key Terms in this Section:**
*   **Square Face:** A facial shape associated with practical, physical, and sometimes stubborn characters.
*   **Motive Temperament:** Focused on physical activity and the skeletal system.
*   **Vital Temperament:** Focused on the digestive and circulatory systems, providing physical vigor.
*   **Religio-Moral Qualities:** The perceived capacity for spirituality, ethics, and empathy.
*   **Selfish Qualities:** Faculties related to self-preservation and personal interest.
*   **Combativeness:** The instinct to defend oneself or meet challenges.
*   **Destructiveness:** The capacity for forceful action or breaking down barriers.

## CHAPTER XVII

### CHINS AND MOUTHS

Physiognomists <term>Physiognomists</term><note>Practitioners of physiognomy, the historical study of determining a person's character or temperament based on their physical features, especially the face.</note> regard the chin as an important feature to be considered in the study of faces as the outer form of character. The following are the principal points of the "reading" of chins.

In Fig. 20 we see the first point to be observed in the study of chins. The rule is to draw an imaginary perpendicular line from the point at the root of the nose, between the two eyebrows. In the normal and average type, the line touches the upper lip and chin. But we find the normal condition in but comparatively few cases, the majority manifesting a variation backward or forward. When the chin is found to recede from the line, it is interpreted as an indication of weakness, lack of stability and firmness, and a general vacillating <note>A term for someone who is indecisive or constantly wavering in their opinions.</note> and unstable character. When the chin projects beyond the line, it is interpreted as indicating firmness, stubbornness, and a generally selfish nature, which is considered "strong" by contrast with the "weak" reced-

# CHINS AND MOUTHS
171

The above points regarding the chin must always be taken into consideration. The following points are based on the shape of the chin when in normal position, that is when the perpendicular line descends in a straight line from the root of the nose to the chin:

The **narrow-round** chin indicates idealistic feeling not manifesting in decided action. These people have high desires, longings, and aspiration, but lack the <term>will</term><note>In the context of historical character analysis, "will" refers to the executive power of the mind—the ability to turn a thought or desire into a physical reality.</note> to act upon the same.

The **narrow-square** chin indicates the idealistic nature, accompanied by the will to act upon the same.

The **broad-round** chin indicates substantial feeling, without the will to manifest it in decided action. These people desire ordinary, plain, practical things, but lack the initiative, will and nerve to overcome obstacles to acquire them.

The **broad-square** chin indicates that the feelings are plain, practical and substantial, with the will to back them up.

From the above, it will be seen that roundness indicates feeling; and that squareness denotes will; that narrowness denotes ideal-

It will be noticed that there is a great difference between the mouth and lips of an individual in childhood, in youth, and in middle-age, which fact shows the truth of the Oriental proverb just quoted. <note>While the specific proverb is not named on this page, 19th-century physiognomists often cited Eastern adages such as "The mouth is the gate of the heart" to justify their theories.</note> The mouth indeed shows what a man has been and is.

*Small mouths* generally denote undeveloped, childish, or babyish character, neither good nor bad.

*Large mouths* denote matured character, good or bad. When firm, they denote force and energy. When half-open, they denote dullness and heaviness. When showing full protruding lips, they denote sensuality and selfish passions and tastes. When very large and flexible, they denote the “windy” person who is fond of talking and hearing the sound of his own voice—when one says that another is “big-mouthed” he states a truth which <term>physiognomy</term><note>Physiognomy: The pseudo-scientific practice of judging a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face.</note> bears out.

Besides the characteristics mentioned above, the student will soon perceive that there are certain "expressions" of the lips and mouth which, although impossible to describe in words, nevertheless may be almost instinctively recognized by the careful observer. Lips, like eyes, tell their story plainly to the practiced observer. It is a safe rule to avoid those whose mouths arouse an instinctive distrust in your mind. # CHAPTER XVIII

## EYES, EARS AND NOSES

It has been said that "the eyes are the windows of the soul," and indeed they do express a *certain quality* <note>original: "something"</note> that is not possible for any other part of the face or body. When unrestrained, the eye correctly portrays the innermost feelings and emotions affecting and influencing us, and in many cases, we are able to get a clear and unobstructed view of the soul behind the eyes by gazing into them. But, alas! it is possible to mask the expression of the eyes and to counterfeit emotions and feelings which do not exist within the mind. Men and women trained in the arts of <term>dissimulation</term><note>The act of concealing one's true thoughts, feelings, or intentions; pretense.</note> and concealment may, and do, hide their thoughts and feelings which would ordinarily be reflected in their eyes; and many, especially women, are able to fake feelings which have no real existence in their minds or souls. We have seen women bestowing upon the unsuspecting "mere man" the most artless, ingenuous "baby stare," while at the same time their minds were filled with craft and cunning.

...safety. The eyes, while full of information, often deceive those not fully versed in their secret code—but the mouth tells its tale in plain, simple, understandable terms, signs, and symbols.

(John D. Rockefeller <note>John D. Rockefeller (1839–1937), the American industrialist, was frequently cited in these texts as a physical example of success and specific character traits.</note> has an almost abnormally long upper lip.)*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the reliability of facial features in reading character, specifically contrasting the deceptive potential of expressions with the structural honesty of the face. It concludes a point about how people can manipulate their gaze to hide their true nature and shifts toward the physical anatomy of the eye area as a more permanent record of a person's habits.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
->HUMAN NATURE<-

...cunning <note>original: "ning"; this concludes the word "cunning" from the previous page.</note>. We have seen others whose eyes portrayed the most absolute innocence and truth, while their hearts were filled with selfish, base feelings, and their minds with cunning schemes. The trained diplomat and skilled gambler successfully mask their thoughts, and their eyes reflect nothing of their secrets; and, upon occasion, they are able to throw into their eyes any desired expression. The best authorities on <term>Physiognomy</term><note>The historical practice of judging a person's character or personality based on their outward appearance, especially the facial features.</note> hold that the mouth is a much more reliable index of thought and character than the eye—for the eye may lie, while the mouth betrays itself even when attempting the counterfeit.

But, nevertheless, the eyes do betray character, not by their expression but by their shape and form. Habitual mental states reflect in the outer form of the eyes, in spite of the care of their owners not to let them tell the secret of the thought and feeling of the moment. The story is told *not* by the expression of the eye, but by the muscles surrounding the eyes, the eye-lids, etc. In fact, the eye-lids supply the greater part of that which we call the "expression of the eye," their contractions and relaxations producing the effect.

**Vocabulary:** Physiognomy, character, eyes, expression, mental states, eye-lids

## EYES, EARS AND NOSES

Secretiveness, cunning, and closeness <note>In this context, "closeness" refers to a guarded or reserved personality.</note> are denoted by closely drawn eye-lids, a furtive look often being imparted thereby. This position of the eye-lids has been likened to an instinctive inclination to draw the eye-lids together to hide the expression of the eye, but it probably arises from the original trait of the animal to protect his eyes from attack when engaging in a fight, or raid. As an instance of this, it will be found that a feeling of cruelty, or desire to hurt another, will manifest in a compression of the eye-lids, and a tightening of the upper eyelid which assumes a straight form. *Frankness, truthfulness and honesty* are, in the same way, indicated by open, free looking eyes. This expression may be counterfeited <note>Meaning faked or simulated to deceive others.</note> upon occasions, but the counterfeit may be detected by observing the eyes when the owner is off guard.

The fighting, destructive, motive feelings are indicated by straight lines of the lids. *Affection, benevolence, sympathy, and love*, manifest in curving, drooping, full eye-lids, the absence of straight lines being marked. <term>Amativeness</term><note>A historical term from phrenology referring to the faculty of physical love or sexual desire.</note> and <term>Alimentiveness</term><note>A term used in character analysis to describe the instinct or appetite for food and drink.</note> show in very thick eye-lids, giving a sensual gross expres-

### EARS

Many <term>physiognomists</term><note>Practitioners of the historical study of judging character or personality from physical features, especially the face.</note> pass lightly over the subject of the ears as an index of character, while others seem to specialize on this feature.

The round ear is held to indicate the <term>Vital Temperament</term><note>A historical classification for a personality type characterized by physical vigor, emotional warmth, and a well-developed digestive system.</note>. The oblong ear, the <term>Motive Temperament</term><note>A classification for a personality type characterized by physical energy, persistence, and a strong-willed nature, often associated with a tall or muscular build.</note>; and the pear-shaped ear the <term>Mental Temperament</term><note>A classification for a personality type characterized by high intellectual capacity, refinement, and sensitivity, often associated with a slight physical build.</note>.

Quality is held to be indicated by the relative delicateness in the moulding of the ear, a coarse, misshapen ear being held to indicate an uncultivated nature; while a delicately moulded, shapely ear is held to indicate culture and refinement.

*   **Combativeness:** Courage and the willingness to confront challenges.

*   **Acquisitiveness:** The impulse to collect or possess.
*   **Constructiveness:** Mechanical or organizational ingenuity.

# CHAPTER XIX

## MISCELLANEOUS SIGNS

While the subject of hands may be thought to belong to the study of <term>Palmistry</term> <note>The practice of claiming to interpret a person's character or predict their future by examining the lines and shapes of their hands.</note>, with which we have no concern in this book, nevertheless we think that we should include herein a brief reference to the several classes of the hand as indicative of the outer form of mental states. That the shape of the hand often reveals information regarding the character of its owner is admitted by the best authorities on the subject. Many persons who discard the theories of Palmistry still regard the subject of the shape and meaning of hands as apart from that study, and believe that an understanding of the indications of the several classes of hands is important to the students of <term>Human Nature</term> <note>In this period, "Human Nature" was often used as a formal term for the study of character through physical traits, similar to physiognomy or phrenology.</note>.

There are seven general types of hands, viz: (1) The <term>Spatulate</term> <note>A hand characterized by fingers that are wide and flat at the tips, resembling a spatula; traditionally associated with people of action, invention, and energy.</note>; (2) the Square; (3) the Artistic; (4) the Elementary; (5) the Mixed; (6) the Philosophic; and (7) the Psychic. Following we give a brief recital of the qualities held to be indicated by each.

***

**Vocabulary for Study**

*   **Palmistry:** The study of the palm's lines, often for fortune-telling.
*   **Human Nature:** The study of personality as revealed by physical appearance.
*   **Spatulate hand:** A hand shape indicating a restless, energetic, and creative disposition.
*   **Square hand:** Typically associated with a practical, orderly, and logical mind.
*   **Artistic hand:** Characterized by tapering fingers, suggesting a love for beauty and sensitivity.
*   **Elementary hand:** A coarse, heavy hand indicating a simpler, more physical nature.
*   **Mixed hand:** A hand showing traits of multiple types, suggesting versatility or inconsistency.
*   **Philosophic hand:** Often bony with prominent joints, indicating a tendency toward deep thought and analysis.
*   **Psychic hand:** A very slender, pointed hand associated with idealism and intuition.

<summary>This page introduces the study of hand shapes as a legitimate branch of character analysis, distinguishing it from the fortune-telling aspects of palmistry. It lists seven distinct types of hands that the author believes reveal the mental and moral state of an individual.</summary>

<keywords>hand types, palmistry, human nature, character analysis, spatulate hand, square hand, philosophic hand, psychic hand</keywords>

Very few hands adhere strictly to any one of these several types, but are more or less <term>composite</term> <note>A "composite" or "mixed" hand combines features from two or more of the standard palmistry types, suggesting a multifaceted personality.</note> or “mixed.” In such cases the characteristics of each type mingle and blend, and must be interpreted accordingly. The following peculiarities are also noted by the authorities:

**THE THUMB.** The thumb is divided into three parts, each indicating a certain quality, as follows: (1) the top part or division, which indicates Will; (2) the second or middle part, which indicates Logic; (3) the “ball” or fat lower portion <note>This is the "Mount of Venus" in traditional palmistry.</note>, which indicates Passion. The comparative size of either of these parts indicates the strength of its particular qualities.

**THE FINGERS.** Hard fingers indicate work, activity, and energy. Soft fingers indicate love of ease, disinclination for work, laziness. Very hard hands denote heaviness and general stupidity <note>In historical character reading, "stupidity" often referred to a perceived lack of refinement or intellectual flexibility rather than a lack of basic intelligence.</note>, also gross tastes and undeveloped nature. Smooth fingers denote artistic tastes, etc.; while <term>knotted fingers</term> <note>Fingers with prominent joints, which were believed to indicate a person who processes information through reason and order rather than intuition.</note> denote philosophic thought and argument, orderliness

**The Palm.** Hardness of the palm, as of the fingers, denotes activity, energy and work; while softness denotes love of ease, laziness, etc. Wideness of the palm denotes generosity, broad-mindedness, etc.; while a narrow palm denotes the reverse. Firm palms generally denote the <term>Motive Temperament</term> <note>A term from the "Doctrine of Temperaments" referring to individuals with strong physical constitutions who are driven by action and willpower.</note>; while soft, flabby palms denote the <term>Vital temperament</term> <note>A temperament characterized by a focus on the "vital" organs—the digestive and circulatory systems—associated with a love of comfort and a sociable, sometimes sluggish, nature.</note>.

### THE WALK

The study of the Walk as an index of character is favored by many authorities. There are three general types of walks, namely <note>original Latin: "viz." (videlicet)</note> (1) the long stride, in regular time; (2) the short, quick, and somewhat jerky step; (3) the short but regular step.

Those who walk with a long stride generally take a broad view of things, but if their walk is also slow they are apt to lack energy

and push. The short, quick step denotes activity, but small ideas and often pettiness. The combination of the long stride and the quick movement is held to indicate both large ideas and activity. A draggy, shuffling walk is held to indicate a careless, shiftless nature; and a springy movement is indicative of mental activity. A mincing walk is held to denote “finickiness,” affectation, and general artificiality; while a careless walk denotes a disregard for appearances and a general unconventional nature. Those who walk in a straight line, direct to the object they seek, are apt to move in the same way in other affairs of life; while those who zig-zag from side to side display the same lack of directness in business affairs and other activities of life. In the same way, one who makes short-cuts across corners, etc., is held to have the same tendency in active affairs.

A little study and observation will convince anyone that the walk and carriage of an individual correspond very closely to his general character. And just as we may recognize one's mental characteristics when reproduced in outer form in the walk; so may we deduce the existence of mental characteristics in a stranger, from the outer form of his walk and carriage. The study of walk and carriage is very interesting, and will repay one for the time and trouble expended upon it. One may

### VOICE

The Voice is a great revealer of character. Prof. O. S. Fowler <note>Orson Squire Fowler (1809–1887) was a prominent American phrenologist and lecturer who argued that a person's physical traits, especially the shape of the skull, were direct reflections of their mental faculties.</note> says: “Whatever makes a noise, from the deafening roar of sea, cataract, and whirlwind’s mighty crash, through all forms of animal life, to the sweet and gentle voice of woman, makes a sound which agrees perfectly with the maker’s character. Thus the terrific roar of the lion, and the soft cooing of the dove, correspond exactly with their respective dispositions; while the rough and powerful bellow of the bull, the fierce yell of the tiger, the coarse, guttural moan of the hyena, the swinish grunt, the sweet warblings of birds, in contrast with the

<summary>This page concludes a lesson on analyzing character through a person's walk and introduces the theory that the human voice is a definitive indicator of personality, drawing parallels between sounds in nature and animal dispositions.</summary>
<keywords>gait, carriage, Prof. O. S. Fowler, character analysis, voice, physiognomy, animal symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues a quotation from Orson Squire Fowler comparing animal sounds to their dispositions and transitions into the phrenological theories of L. A. Vaught regarding how the voice originates from different regions of the brain and skull.</meta>

# HUMAN NATURE

raven’s croak and the owl’s hoot, each correspond perfectly with their respective characteristics. And this law holds equally true of man. Hence human intonations are as superior to <term>brutal</term> <note>In this 19th-century context, "brutal" refers specifically to the nature of "brutes" (animals) rather than implying intentional cruelty.</note> as human character exceeds animal. Accordingly, the peculiarities of all human beings are expressed in their voices and mode of speaking. Coarse-grained and powerful animal organizations have a coarse, harsh and grating voice, while in exact proportion as persons become refined and elevated mentally, will their tones of voice become correspondingly refined and perfected.”

Prof. L. A. Vaught <note>Louis Allen Vaught (1859–1910) was a prominent American author who promoted "character reading," a practice that combined phrenology—the study of skull shapes—with the study of facial features and body language.</note> says: “Affectionate voices always come from the <term>backhead</term> <note>The posterior part of the skull, which phrenologists believed was the seat of social and domestic affections.</note>. Heavy, thunderous voices always come from the <term>sidehead</term> <note>The area above the ears, associated by phrenologists with "executive" faculties such as energy, combativeness, and self-preservation.</note>. Egotistical voices come from the <term>crown</term> of the head <note>The highest point of the back of the head, associated with the faculties of Self-Esteem and Firmness.</note>. Kind, respectful and straightforward voices come from the <term>top-head</term> <note>The superior region of the skull, which phrenologists identified as the location of moral and religious faculties like benevolence and veneration.</note>.”

A clear, distinct utterance is held to indicate clear, logical thought, while indistinct, confused, slurring utterance is indicative of careless, illogical and hasty thought processes. Sharp and shrill notes denote nervous tension and lack of restraint, as witness

->### EYES, EARS AND NOSES<-

the voice of the shrew <note>A historical term for a woman perceived as being of violent temper and speech.</note> or the hysterical woman <note>In the late 19th and early 20th centuries, "hysteria" was a common medical diagnosis used to categorize various emotional or physical symptoms in women.</note>, or the high-strung nervous man. Self-restraint is shown by calm, deep, forceful utterances. Slowness in delivery denotes slow, deliberate mental processes, while quickness, and “snappiness” in speech, denotes quick, active habits of thought. The cheerful voice of the optimistic person, and the rasping whine of the chronic pessimist, are well known. The voice of self-reliance, and the voice of fear and lack of self-esteem, are easily recognized. The strident, overconfident note of the boaster and vain-glorious person, is easily distinguished from that of the modest, careful, reliable person.

All the several mental Qualities manifest in the voice, in tone, pitch or feeling. The **Emotive Qualities** <note>The faculties related to feelings, affections, and emotions.</note> give the affectionate voice; **Self-Esteem** gives the confident voice; **Approbativeness** <note>A phrenological term referring to the desire for approval, praise, or social status.</note> gives the voice of affectation and conceit; **Combativeness** <note>The faculty associated with courage, resistance, and the impulse to overcome opposition.</note> gives the “let me alone” tone; **Destructiveness** <note>In phrenology, this faculty represents force, energy, and severity; in the voice, it manifests as a harsh or aggressive tone.</note> gives the “get out of my way” note; **Cunning** and **Acquisitiveness** <note>The faculty related to the desire for possession and profit.</note> give the tone of deceit and flattery; and so on, through the entire scale. ### LAUGHS

Laughter is full of the expression of character. One may often accurately determine the character of a person whose face is not seen or known. A hearty laugh is indicative of sympathy, companionship and general sociability, as well as a well developed sense of humor. A giggle is indicative of pettiness, trifling and general mental light-weight <note>A historical idiom for a person perceived as lacking intellectual depth or serious moral character.</note>. The repressed laugh shows self-control and often caution and cunning, the tone denoting the difference. The vulgar "haw-haw" denotes a correspondingly gross nature. The peculiar shrill, rasping, parrot-like laugh of the courtesan <note>In this period, "courtesan" was often used as a euphemism for a woman of "disreputable" character; the author suggests that a specific vocal quality can betray a hardened or deceptive nature.</note> is typical, and when ever heard should act as a note of warning. It is difficult to state in words the various qualities of the laugh, but each is distinctive and well expresses the Quality <note>In phrenology and physiognomy, a "Quality" refers to a specific faculty or trait of the mind supposed to be located in a particular part of the brain.</note> causing it. It may be said that each and every mental Quality has

### THE HAND-SHAKE

The manner of shaking hands is indicative of the characteristics of the individual. Handshakes may be divided into three general classes, namely: (1) the hearty handshake, which indicates good-feeling, earnestness, and interest; (2) the mechanical handshake, which denotes indifference, lack of feeling, and lack of interest; and (3) the selfish handshake, which denotes cunning, heartlessness, and a desire and disposition to take advantage of the other party. There is a “something” in the handshake which is almost impossible to express in words, but which is recognized instinctively by those having Human Nature <note>In the phrenological system used by the author, "Human Nature" is considered a specific mental faculty—usually located at the top of the forehead—that allows a person to intuitively "read" the character and motives of others.</note> well developed. It is more of a “feeling” of certain Qualities <note>In this context, "Qualities" refers to the various mental powers or faculties of the mind that phrenologists believed were manifested through physical actions and bodily form.</note> manifested by the other person. A little thought and attention paid to this subject will tend to develop this recognition on the part of one deficient in it. One may, with a little practice, learn to distinguish between the honest and the dis- <note>The text ends mid-word, likely intended to be "dishonest" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the handshake as a key indicator of personality, categorizing it into three types—hearty, mechanical, and selfish—based on the underlying character traits they reveal.</summary>
<keywords>handshake, Human Nature faculty, character analysis, social signs, phrenology, interpersonal intuition</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on handshakes, completing the sentence from the previous page which contrasted the "honest" and "dishonest" handshake. It serves as the final summary of the author’s methodology for character reading.</meta>

202
->HUMAN NATURE<-

honest; the moral and the immoral; the active and the passive; the energetic and the slothful; the grasp of good-fellowship, and that of superciliousness; the friendly and the antagonistic; the candid and the deceitful; and all the other various kinds of handshakes. Mental states manifest in outer form in hand-shakes as in many other physical actions and appearances.

First study the several Qualities <note>In the phrenological and physiognomical systems of the time, "Qualities" referred to the distinct mental faculties or "organs" of the brain that dictated personality traits.</note> in their inner aspect, and then learn to distinguish the various outer forms of each. From the inner proceed to the outer, and having learned the way you will be able to retrace your steps from the outer to the inner in the case of other persons. The principle once grasped, the rest is all a matter of practice and experience.

->THE END<-
<note>original: "FINIS"</note>
